## A2: the decisions that are yours

The brief fixes the four changes and the windows. It leaves six things open, and each one changes what the table says. I have a lean on each, marked as such, but I am not proceeding on any of them until you say.

**D1. Where deduplication sits.** The analyst's 0.967 is on the raw 33,340 rows. Dedup is not one of the four changes, yet every row after it will be on 31,645.
- (a) Row 0 is the analyst as-is on raw rows (reproduces 0.967 and proves the harness). Then a labelled "row 0, deduplicated" before change 1, so the dedup effect is visible on its own line.
- (b) Dedup silently, and row 0 is the analyst's protocol on clean rows. The 0.967 is then a number in the exhibit that the table never reproduces.
- (c) Fold dedup into change 2, on the argument that a duplicate row in both folds is the extreme case of "one member, many rows."
- *Lean:* (a). It costs one row, keeps "one change at a time" literal, and the harness reproducing 0.967 is the check that everything downstream is measured against the right starting point.

**D2. Order of the four changes.** This one has a structural constraint, not just a preference. Once change 3 is made, there is no cross-validation any more: the model is fit on months to June 2025 and scored on October to December. Members do appear on both sides of that boundary, and that is *correct*, because in April 2026 the model will have been fit on the past rows of the very members it scores. So change 2 (grouped folds) has nothing to act on after change 3. Two consequences:
- Change 2 must come before change 3, or its row shows exactly zero by construction rather than by finding.
- The brief says "from change 3 onward it is measured on the test window." The later change 3 comes, the fewer rows touch the test set. With order 1, 2, 3, 4 the test window is scored once, at row 3, and row 4 is those same scores split by segment. With change 3 earlier, subsequent rows re-score the test window. That is not selection, since the changes are prescribed, but it is closer to the line than it needs to be.
- Remaining choice is whether 1 or 2 goes first. 1 → 2 → 3 → 4 fixes the model's inputs first and then the folds; 2 → 1 → 3 → 4 measures the column leak under honest folds. Either is defensible and the memo must say why. This is also the rubric's "not an order-independent decomposition" point: the size of change 1's drop depends on whether the folds are still leaking when you measure it.
- *Lean:* 1 → 2 → 3 → 4, the brief's own order. The columns are the one thing that is wrong at the level of the data rather than the protocol, and everything after it is then measured on a model that could actually be deployed.

**D3. Which columns are admissible.** The dictionary is explicit for most, and every kept column needs the same one-line justification as every dropped one (rubric A2).
- Twelve have timing notes that place them at or before the start of the month: `tenure_months` ("as of the start of snapshot_month"), the six 30-day behavioural columns and `payment_failures_last_90d` (windows ending on the last day of the previous month), `acquisition_channel` and `city_tier` (fixed at signup), `plan_tier` and `monthly_price` (billing).
- Two are recorded during or after: `winback_offer_sent` (list circulated after month close) and `exit_survey_started` (the cancellation flow; A1 showed it is 1 only on last rows).
- `member_id`, `snapshot_month`, `signup_month` stay out as the analyst had them. Month cannot be a feature in any case: April 2026 is a value the model has never seen.
- The one to argue rather than assert: `plan_tier` and `monthly_price` have no timing note. If a member downgrades mid-month, does the row show the old plan or the new one? The dictionary does not say. Keeping them is the reasonable reading of "billing snapshot," and the memo should say that is a reading.
- Also worth one sentence: keeping `acquisition_channel` when the model has seen corporate members for at most six months. It is admissible and informative; the segment row is where its weakness shows.
- *Lean:* twelve in, two out, with the plan-price caveat stated.

**D4. How grouped folds are built for change 2.**
- `StratifiedGroupKFold(5, shuffle=True, random_state=0)`: keeps the class balance per fold like the analyst's `StratifiedKFold`, adds only the grouping. Closest to "one change."
- `GroupKFold(5)`: simpler, no stratification, no seed.
- *Lean:* `StratifiedGroupKFold`, same seed as the analyst, so the row differs from row 1 in the grouping alone.

**D5. What change 3 does with the validation month.** A2 makes no data-driven choice (logistic regression, no hyperparameters), so 2025-09 sits unused. Options: leave it unused and say so; or report a labelled "validation-window" AUC row as a dress rehearsal before the test row. Not required. It may matter in A3 if you want to check calibration somewhere other than the test set before deriving the threshold from costs.
- *Lean:* report it as an extra labelled row. It is cheap and it gives A3 a place to look that is not the test set.

**D6. Change 4 counts.** Corporate churners in the test window will be few (66 in the whole extract). The rubric asks for row and churner counts beside every segment AUC, and with few positives the AUC will be noisy. The choice is whether to add any uncertainty measure (a bootstrap CI on the segment AUC) or to let the counts speak.
- *Lean:* counts only in A2, and decide on a CI when you see how small the corporate churner count is.

**Fixed by the brief, no decision:** windows (train to 2025-06, skip July and August, validate 2025-09, test from 2025-10), `analyst_pipeline()` imported unmodified from the supplied file, the test window scored once.

Two rubric items you will need to write in your own words and I will not draft: why you chose the order, and why the two-month gap exists. On the gap, one pointer only: look at what date a June 2025 label is determined by, and what date a September 2025 feature is measured through.

Give me D1 through D6 and I will build `a2_evaluation.py`.

Policy line 47: *"Using AI to help you draft the memo, provided the judgments in it are yours and you can defend them."* So drafting is permitted. Your `CLAUDE.md` is stricter than the policy ("never write the judgment"), and it is read at the start of every session, so if you want the relaxed rule to stick, that line needs changing. I will draft prose and exhibits on request. For the recommendation itself and any "good enough" verdict, I will ask you to state the judgment first and then put it into words, because the policy's condition is that the judgment is yours and the oral check applies to every line. Say if you want me to edit `CLAUDE.md` to say exactly that.

Now the questions, in order.

## D2: what "held-out" means, and cumulative

Yes, cumulative. Each row adds one change to everything above it.

"Held-out" has three meanings in this assignment, and change 2 is about the first:

1. **The held-out fold inside cross-validation.** Rows 0 to 2 are 5-fold CV: each fold is fitted on four fifths and scored on the fifth. Change 2 says a member's rows must all land in the same fifth, so the model never scores a member it has already seen rows of. That is `StratifiedGroupKFold` with `groups=member_id`, and that settles D4.
2. **The validation month, 2025-09.** Reserved for choices. Not a fold.
3. **The test window, 2025-10 to 12.** Scored once, at row 3.

After change 3 there are no folds, so change 2 has nothing to act on. Members *will* straddle the June 2025 boundary, and that is deployment-realistic: in April 2026 the model is fit on the past rows of the members it scores. The alternative reading, "restrict the test window to members never seen in training," answers a different question (how does it do on brand-new members?) and would leave very few test rows. Your call whether to report that as a diagnostic; I would not put it in the main table.

## D5: why 2025-09 stays unused in A2

It is not in the folds, correct. It is reserved for "any choice made by looking at data." In A2 there is no such choice: the model is logistic regression with no hyperparameters, the columns are decided from the dictionary, and the four changes are prescribed. So there is nothing to select and the month sits idle. My lean was to score it anyway as a labelled "validation-window" row, a dress rehearsal for the test row, and to give A3 a place to check calibration before the threshold is applied to the test set. It selects nothing in A2. Your call.

## D6: bootstrap CI

An AUC on a segment with, say, 25 churners is a noisy number. A bootstrap confidence interval tells you how noisy: resample the test rows with replacement, compute the AUC on the resample, repeat 1,000 times, and take the 2.5th and 97.5th percentiles of the results. The interval says "AUCs in this range are consistent with the data." One detail: because a member's rows are correlated, the honest version resamples *members*, not rows. Part B's B3 uses the same idea, so it is not a new concept for you later. Decide after you see how many corporate churners the test window has.

## The two-month gap

Your explanation has the right mechanism; here is the precise version. A June 2025 training row's label is determined by whether the member cancels by 31 August 2025. So training labels encode July and August outcomes. If July or August were validation months, the outcomes being predicted would already sit inside the training labels of the same members' June rows. The gap ends training at the last month whose labels have matured before validation starts. It also mirrors deployment: on 1 April 2026 the two most recent months of labels do not exist yet either. And since A1 showed the label actually flags only the final month, the gap is conservative, which you already noted.

## D7: a decision I missed, "building data"

The brief says rows 0 to 2 are "cross-validated on the building data." That phrase decides which rows go into the folds:

- (a) **All 24 months**, like the analyst. Row 0 then matches the analyst's protocol exactly except dedup. But CV scores every row out-of-fold, including October to December 2025, so the test window has been scored three times before row 3 "scores it once."
- (b) **Training window only, to 2025-06.** Test and validation are never touched before row 3. Row 0 then differs from "as printed" in two ways at once: dedup and window. The raw-file harness check covers the wiring; a footnote covers the rest.
- (c) **Everything except the test window**, to 2025-09. Middle ground with no obvious rationale.

*Lean:* (b). "Score the test set once" is a ground rule, and (b) is the only option that keeps it literal. If you want the dedup and window effects separated, row 0 can be two labelled lines, "all months, deduplicated" and "building window," at the cost of one extra row.

## What I have from you so far

Settled: dedup before row 0 with a raw-file harness check; D3 twelve in, two out, plan-price caveat, channel kept. Waiting on: D2 order (1→2→3→4 unless you say otherwise), D5 validation row yes or no, D7 building data, and whether the straddling diagnostic is wanted. D4 and D6 resolve themselves from those.

                  \"protocol\": protocol, \"rows_fit\": n_fit, \"rows_scored\": n_scored,\n                     \"churners_scored\": k_scored, \"auc\": round(auc, 4), \"kind\": kind})\n Harness check reproduced 0.967 on the raw file, so the wiring is right. Row 0 at 0.968 says dedup and the window restriction change nothing on their own, which is what you would expect when the leak is still in.

The brief said one change would move the number a great deal and one barely at all. Change 1 dropped 0.22. Change 2 dropped 0.001. Change 3 then took another 0.10, which the brief did not promise and which is the interesting one.

## Before you interpret: three things I want to argue against

**Row 3 is below A1's single-column baselines.** The 12-column model ranks the test window at 0.644. In A1, `avg_session_minutes` alone ranked all rows at 0.681. Those are different rows, so it is not a fair comparison yet, but if one column *on the test window* also gets 0.68, then the fitted model is adding nothing over a rule the retention team could apply by hand, and the VP should hear that. The check would score the test window with a second rule. It selects nothing, but it is a second look at the test set, and the ground rule is "score once." Your call whether that reference belongs in the exhibit, in a footnote, or nowhere.

**Corporate at 0.404 is not just noise.** Below 0.5 means the model ranks corporate churners *lower* than corporate non-churners. With 34 churners among 860 rows, the standard error of an AUC near 0.5 is roughly 0.05, so 0.40 is about two standard errors off. Something structural is plausible: the model saw 533 corporate rows with at most six months of tenure, learned that corporate means low churn (3.1%), and whatever drives churn *within* the corporate segment (an employer contract ending, say) is not in any column. It is also 34 churners, which is going to make the corporate line of A3's dollar estimate fragile whatever the AUC says.

**The pooled test number hides this.** Corporate is 19% of test rows but 8% of test churners, so the pooled 0.644 is essentially the "everyone else" 0.641. A book that is 35% corporate in April 2026 will not be described by 0.644.

One optional diagnostic that would help you explain row 4 rather than speculate: the fitted model's coefficients as odds ratios (Handout 2 §7.2 is on the reading list for exactly this). It would show what the model does with `acquisition_channel = corporate` and which behavioural columns carry the ranking. No test-set involvement. Say if you want it.

## First, a correction to your opening question

Rows 0 to 2 are *also* scored on unseen data. In 5-fold CV each fifth is scored by a model that never saw it. So "row 3 dropped because the test set is unseen" is not the explanation. What changes at row 3 is *which* unseen data: rows 0 to 2 hold out random fifths of the same months, row 3 holds out a later period. The 0.10 drop is the cost of time, not of novelty. That is the sentence for row 3.

## Odds ratios

Logistic regression fits log-odds, not probability: log(p / (1 − p)) = β₀ + β₁x₁ + … Exponentiating a coefficient gives an **odds ratio**: the factor by which the odds of churn multiply when that feature goes up by one unit, holding the others fixed. OR above 1 raises churn odds, below 1 lowers them, 1 means no effect. Two things specific to this pipeline: numeric columns were standardised, so "one unit" is one standard deviation of the building data (the table also gives per raw unit); one-hot columns are relative to the dropped first category.

What the row-3 model learned, from `a2_odds_ratios.csv`, largest first:

| Feature | OR | Read as |
|---|---:|---|
| channel = partner_gym / paid_search / organic, vs **corporate** | 4.3 / 4.1 / 4.1 | A non-corporate member has about **four times** the churn odds of a corporate member, everything else equal. The largest thing in the model. |
| plan_tier = elite vs basic | 0.38 | Elite members have 38% of basic's churn odds |
| sessions_last_30d | 0.57 per SD (4.5 sessions); 0.88 per session | Each extra session cuts churn odds by 12% |
| avg_session_minutes | 0.61 per SD | Longer sessions, lower churn |
| monthly_price | 1.38 per SD | Higher price, higher churn |
| plan_tier = plus vs basic | 0.75 | |
| days_since_last_login | 1.33 per SD | |
| tenure_months | 0.85 per SD (9.6 months) | Weak, consistent with A1's flat table |
| app_opens_last_30d | 1.12 per SD | See caution below |
| support tickets, referrals, payment failures, city tier | 0.9 to 1.1 | Near nothing |

Three cautions before you quote any of these:

1. **`plan_tier` and `monthly_price` are collinear** (tier determines list price) and their signs oppose: elite lowers churn, price raises it. That is the classic signature of two collinear columns splitting one effect. Read them together, not separately.
2. **`app_opens_last_30d` flipped sign.** In A1, fewer opens meant more churn (single-column AUC 0.648 with that direction). Here, *given* sessions and minutes, more opens go with slightly more churn. Opening the app without working out may be a bad sign. It is also a reminder that a single-variable direction and a multivariable coefficient answer different questions.
3. **The corporate finding is learned from 533 rows with at most six months of tenure.** The channel dummy shifts every corporate member's score down by the same amount and then leaves the behavioural columns to rank within the segment. Row 4 says those columns rank corporate churners *backwards* (0.40). Whatever drives churn inside the corporate segment is not in these twelve columns, or it points the other way there. The odds ratios do not tell you which; they tell you the model has no corporate-specific signal to use.

## Your three answers

**1. Row 0. Wrong.** 0.968 against 0.967 is the same number, not "better," and the two are on different rows (building window versus all months) so even the third decimal is not comparable. Dedup did not fix anything measurable because with `exit_survey_started` still in, the model reaches 0.97 with or without duplicated rows. The sentence: *deduplication and restricting to the building window leave the number where it was; the leak dominates everything else.* The brief says reporting a near-zero effect correctly earns the same marks as a large one. This is that row.

**2. Order. Mostly right, three fixes.**
- Change 2's main purpose in CV is not "new clients at deployment," although that is true too. It is that with a member's other rows in the fitting fold, the model can recognise the member instead of learning a pattern, and the CV number overstates. Say that.
- Your change-3 example is good, and the A1 label finding makes it exact: seeing a member's *later* row in training tells you their earlier rows are all 0, because the flag only ever sits on the last row.
- You left out the structural reason: 2 must precede 3 because after 3 there are no folds to group; and 3 comes as late as possible so the test window is scored once, at row 3, with row 4 splitting those same scores. Those two facts *are* the order justification. Put them first.
- For row 4, describe what the row measures ("the same scores, split by channel, with counts") rather than what you expected to find. The finding is the number.

**3. Order-independence. Wrong, and here is the number.** Dedup does not make member overlap rare. Under the analyst's random split on the building window, **2,899 of 3,648 members (79.5%) have rows in two or more folds.** Member overlap is fully present at row 1, change 2 removes all of it, and the number moves 0.001. It moves so little because the twelve admissible columns cannot identify a member: they are monthly behaviour and a handful of fixed attributes shared by thousands of people, and a linear model has no way to memorise "this is member 4471" from them. The leaking columns were what made recognition trivial, and they were already gone.

That last clause is the order-independence answer. Change 2's 0.001 is its effect *given that change 1 has already been made*. The cleaner illustration is change 3 against change 1: `exit_survey_started` is 1 on every churner's last row in every period, so if past-to-future had been done first with the leaking columns still in, the test AUC would have stayed near 0.97 and "time" would have looked free; dropping the columns afterwards would then have taken the whole fall, including the 0.10 we now attribute to time. Same total drop, completely different split. The rows are a path, not a decomposition.

If you want that made concrete without touching the test window, I can run the CV-only alternative (change 2 before change 1) as a diagnostic. Not required; your reasoning is enough for the rubric.

Ready to draft the A2 prose whenever you say. What I have from you that goes in: the order justification (with the two structural facts added), the row-3 "time not novelty" reading, the gap sentence with the deployment mirror, and the corporate reading above. What I still want in your words: one sentence for row 1 and for row 4.

ASSISTANT  [2026-09-21T04:32:00.847Z]
==============================================================================

Two things you said need adjusting, then the number, then the draft.

**"Time seems to be a strong predictor."** Time is not a predictor and the temporal split adds no information to the model. What change 3 does is *measure* something the random split could not: how well a model fit on the past ranks a later period. The 0.10 is the cost of drift, revealed, not a feature contributing. Say "the temporal split reveals" rather than "chronology predicts."

**"Change 2 is the weakest because this is a linear model."** Right conclusion, slightly wrong reason. It is weak because the twelve admissible columns contain nothing that identifies a member, and a linear model cannot manufacture identity out of shared attributes. A flexible model (a tree ensemble, say) *can* partly memorise members from combinations of channel, tier, city, price and tenure trajectory, so for that model change 2 would matter more. Two consequences for the memo: change 2 was still the right thing to do, and its near-zero effect is a fact about this model and these columns, not evidence the change was unnecessary. Also, you are right that in this dataset change 2 would be near zero in *any* position: with the leaking columns in, the AUC is pinned at 0.97 by `exit_survey_started` regardless of grouping; with them out, there is nothing to memorise. That is a coincidence of this data, not a general rule.

Your intuition about the linear model and the leak is exactly right otherwise: the leaking columns were the only thing simple enough for a linear model to exploit.

**The 79.5%.** It came from a scratch run, not from `run.sh`, so it cannot go in the memo yet. Fixing that now.

ASSISTANT  [2026-09-21T04:39:39.859Z]
==============================================================================

Simpler version.

A **predictor** is a column the model reads: sessions, tenure, channel. Row 2 and row 3 use the *same twelve columns*. Change 3 does not add a column called "time" to the model. The model at row 3 has no idea what month it is.

What change 3 changes is **which rows are used to fit and which rows are used to score**:

- Rows 0 to 2: shuffle all the building-window rows, fit on four fifths, score the other fifth. The fitting rows and the scoring rows come from the *same months*, mixed together. January 2024 and June 2025 are on both sides.
- Row 3: fit on everything up to June 2025, score October to December 2025. The scoring rows come from *after* every fitting row.

Same model, same columns, different exam.

An analogy. A student studies from a pile of past exam papers. Test one: pull 20 random questions out of that same pile and see how many they get right. Test two: give them next year's exam, written after the syllabus changed a bit. The student is the same person with the same knowledge. If they score lower on test two, it is not because "next year" is something the student knows about or uses. It is because test two asks a harder and more honest question: how well does what you learned hold up when the world has moved on?

That is what the 0.10 is. In Jan 2024 to Jun 2025 the relationship between the columns and churn is one thing. In Oct to Dec 2025 the book is 19% corporate, a competitor has been spending since July, and the season is different. The random split cannot see any of this because both sides of every fold contain the same mixture of months, so whatever the relationship was, it was the same on both sides. The temporal split puts the old relationship on the fitting side and the new one on the scoring side, and the gap between them shows up as a lower AUC.

So:

- "Time is a strong predictor" would mean the model uses the date to predict churn. It does not.
- "The temporal split reveals a 0.10 cost" means: when asked the deployment question, how well does a model fit on the past rank the future, the answer is 0.10 lower than the random split suggested. The random split was answering a question nobody at Northline is asking. April 2026 is always the future.

One test of whether it has landed: if there were *no* drift at all, if the book, the competitor and the season had all stayed exactly the same, what would row 3 have shown relative to row 2?

# AI process note — raw material (draft, not the deliverable)

The submitted `ai_process_note.md` is one page max, in my own words, covering
both parts. This file collects the per-session material so nothing is lost.
Append a block per session; condense at the end.

Rubric reminder: "Generic notes score zero. 'Used Claude Code to write the
analysis, reviewed the output, verified it was correct' is a generic note."
Name the specific tasks, the specific rejections with reasons, and the
specific checks.

---

## Session 2 (2026-09-17, macOS) — concept review and A1

**Delegated:** Mac environment build; `analysis/run.sh`, the A1 script and
its README; reading the handouts and locating where each concept lives;
drafting the A1 exhibit and prose; the end-of-session export and notes.

**Changed or rejected:**
- Agent called the label-structure finding (one flagged row per churner,
  always the last) "interesting." I pushed back — obviously the cancellation
  month is the last month. That produced the sharper statement: the
  dictionary's 60-day window would flag two rows per churner, the extract
  flags one, so the label is really "cancellation month," and the leak is a
  same-row timing problem, not a look-ahead into later rows.
- I reframed the A1 "what would have to be true" paragraph around "the data
  dictionary's timing notes would have to be correct, and the columns not
  back-filled from later months." That is now the lead of the paragraph.
- I chose four single-variable columns (days since login, tenure, app opens,
  session minutes) and both row-level and member-level base rates; the agent
  had proposed one column and row-level only.
- I asked whether the leakage evidence (exit-survey / winback cross-tabs)
  was produced by the run. It was not — it came from a one-off check in the
  session. It was added to the script (`a1_column_timing.csv`) before it was
  allowed into the draft.
- Agent replaced `.vscode/settings.json`, which had been pointing VS Code at
  the system interpreter instead of `.venv`; I asked for that fix.
- I asked whether to drop the brief's two-month gap given the label behaves
  like a 30-day window. Decision: keep it — the brief fixes the windows for
  comparability, and the protocol should be safe under the documented
  definition, not the reverse-engineered one.
- Agent had said the cross-validation material was in "Handout 1 §6" per the
  brief; on reading the PDFs it is Handout 2 §4. Recorded the mapping so
  citations in the memo are right.

**How numbers were checked:**
- Base rate predicted at ~9% before running (from the brief); came out 9.29%.
- Member-level 70% reconciled against the row-level rate: a ~4.5%/month
  hazard compounds to ~67% over 24 months, so both are consistent.
- The exit-survey AUC of 0.808 recomputed by hand as coverage plus half the
  ties: 0.616 + 0.5 × 0.384 = 0.808. Same for winback: 0.858.
- The analyst's 0.967 comes from running their script untouched and capturing
  its output, not from retyping the brief's figure.
- One-off diagnostic numbers were accepted only once the script reproduced
  them exactly (dedup count, monthly rates, corporate share, the cross-tabs).
- Everything came out as expected, which the course doc says is the moment to
  slow down; the one unexpected thing (label structure) was found by checking
  rather than trusting the dictionary.

**Would still not bet on:** whether the July–August 2025 churn spike is the
competitor or seasonality — 2024 also peaked in July–September, and two years
cannot separate them. Memo says "coinciding with," not "caused by."

--- NOTES.md sessions 4-5 ---
## Session 4 — 2026-09-29 (macOS laptop). A3 built and drafted. Part A complete.

Transcript: `sessions/claude-code/a1-session-4.jsonl` and `.txt`.

### What was done

- Pulled session 3 from Windows. **Cross-machine reproducibility verified**:
  re-running everything on the Mac left `a2_table.csv` and all six A1 outputs
  byte-identical. Only `a2_test_scores.csv` moved, by at most 4e-14 in
  `p_churn` (ARM vs x86 floating point), and the *same 203 rows* cleared the
  threshold on both machines, so no dollar figure depends on the laptop.
  `run.log` differs only by Python patch version and path separators.
- Verified A2's partitions against the brief's window table from the data, not
  from reading the code: fitted on 2024-01..2025-06 (18 months), scored on
  2025-10..12 (3 months), 2025-07/08/09 in neither, no month in both.
- `analysis/part1/a3_decision.py` written and added to `run.sh`. Full run is
  ~12 s. Threshold derivation, calibration check and correction, value
  exhibit, monthly split, offer-quality table, value curve and figure.
- `drafts/a3_draft.md`: recommendation, Exhibits A3a-A3d, prose (448 words),
  notes, argue-against list, provenance table, trimming candidates.

### Design decisions made (mine)

- One offer = one member-month (matches the monthly programme and the brief's
  per-offer economics; per-member accounting has no honest answer to "which
  month did the offer happen in", since a churner has only one flagged row).
- Quarter as the reporting period. Monthly split added afterwards once it
  proved informative — I had decided to skip it.
- Priced t* against offer-everyone, no programme, and the analyst's 0.267.
  Declined to score the test window with the analyst's *model*: that is a
  second scoring, and A2 set the norm against it.
- Calibration: checked on the validation month, and corrected there with
  Platt scaling. **The verdict was set from validation evidence before any
  dollar figure existed.** It cost $30.
- Value curve reported (re-reads the same scores, no new scoring).
- Recommendation shape (scope to non-corporate + dollar tripwire) chosen
  before the numbers were computed.
- Declined to add window-compliance assertions to `a2_evaluation.py`: A2 is
  run and answered.

### Numbers produced, with provenance (all from `bash analysis/run.sh`)

| Quantity | Value | File |
|---|---|---|
| Threshold t* | 0.2143 = 18/84, two routes agreeing | `a3_threshold.txt` |
| Value at t*, quarter | $852 (242 offers, 62 to churners, 180 wasted) | `a3_value.csv` |
| Standard error of that total | $572, i.e. 1.5 SE from zero | `run.log` |
| By segment | corporate -$78 (9 offers, 1 churner); other +$930 (233, 61) | `a3_value.csv` |
| Offer to everyone | -$47,736 (4,556 offers, 408 churners) | " |
| No programme | $0 | " |
| At the analyst's 0.267 | $828 (108 offers, 33 churners) | " |
| Monthly at t* | Oct $48 (114 offers, 21.9%); Nov $810 (81, 33.3%); Dec -$6 (47, 21.3%) | `a3_by_month.csv` |
| First vs repeat contact | $3.97 vs $1.66 per offer; 195 vs 47 offers | `a3_offer_quality.csv` |
| Top-quartile over-prediction | claims 39.6%, delivers 27.9% (61 offers) | " |
| Q1 just above t* | 22.2% claimed, 18.0% observed, -$2.85 per offer | " |
| Validation calibration | slope 0.788, intercept -0.219; under-predicts in 8 of 10 bins; val AUC 0.695 | `a3_calibration.csv`, `run.log` |
| Calibration sensitivity | raw scores give $882 on 203 offers | `run.log` |
| Curve peak | $1,098 at threshold 0.236; not selecting cost $264 | `a3_value_curve.csv` |

Hand arithmetic, stated as such: break-even precision = t* = 21.43%, and
value per offer = 84 x (precision - 0.2143); quarter precision 25.6%.

### Findings this session

- **The margin is thin, not the ranking.** Value per offer is $3.52 against a
  break-even precision of 21.4% on a 9% base rate. Two of the three test
  months are at or below break-even; the quarter's $852 is essentially
  November. With 47-114 offers a month those differences are mostly noise,
  which is the finding: the programme sits close enough to zero that ordinary
  variation straddles it.
- **The threshold was not the analyst's main error.** Their grid-searched
  0.267 on honest scores gives $828 against our derived $852. The $19,774 came
  from leaked columns and a shuffled evaluation, not from the cut point.
- **The model overstates the members it selects.** Top-scoring quartile claims
  39.6% and delivers 27.9% — selecting the top of a noisy score. This is why
  the tripwire is a dollar figure, not AUC: AUC can hold while calibration
  drifts and the cut point stops meaning what it meant.
- A fixed threshold is self-regulating *if* the probabilities are honest, so
  a lower April base rate should mean fewer offers rather than worse ones.
  The exposure is miscalibration under drift, not the base rate itself.

### Open questions / next session (Part B)

1. **Part B is untouched and worth 35 points.** Handout 3 and R2 unread.
   Output-folder question from session 1 still open (`sweep_outputs/` and
   `b3_outputs/` under `analysis/part2/` vs `analysis/outputs/`).
2. **Prose budget is over.** A1 499 + A2 552 + A3 448 = 1,499 words for a
   two-page §1 (~1,000-1,200). Trimming candidates are listed at the end of
   `drafts/a3_draft.md`; roughly 450 words need to come out.
3. `memo.md` not started. `ai_process_note.md` not started; per-session raw
   material is in `drafts/ai_process_note_draft.md` (session 4 block added).
4. Deadline Thursday 1 October, 6:00 PM Central.

### Two structural points for the write-up (settled end of session 4)

- **The memo is not the drafts stapled together.** One document, two
  sections: §1 to the Northline VP (A1-A3, max 2 pages of prose, Exhibits
  A1-A3), §2 to the Cascade VP (B1-B3, max 1 page, Exhibits B1-B3). Each
  section *leads with its recommendation* and works backwards through the
  evidence. The drafts are organised by rubric item so nothing is lost; the
  memo has to be reorganised by argument. Some of the 450-word trim comes
  free from that, since the three A-drafts repeat context for each other.
  Also check the exhibit count: eight labels exist (A1, A2, A2b, A2c,
  A3a-A3d) against the brief's "Exhibits A1-A3"; sub-lettering is fine but
  four under A3 is probably one or two too many.
- **`ai_process_note.md` is one page maximum for both parts**, stated in
  README, the submission checklist and course doc 01, and length limits are
  "stated per assignment, and enforced". That is ~500 words against the
  1,927 now in `drafts/ai_process_note_draft.md`, with Part B still to add.
  Treat that file as a quarry, not a first draft; it is not submitted. The
  strongest items are the ones where I corrected the agent, not the ones
  where it built something. Write it last, in one pass, covering both parts.

### For office hours (added session 5, before 2026-09-30)

Three questions, ranked by how much they could move a grade.

1. **The "building data" ambiguity in A2.** The brief says rows before change
   3 are "cross-validated on the building data" without defining the phrase.
   We read it as the training window (months <= 2025-06, 21,031 rows). The
   alternative reading is everything that is not test (<= 2025-09, including
   the two gap months and the validation month). If the second reading is
   intended, rows 0-2 change and `a2_evaluation.py` needs a one-line edit and
   a re-run -- not a rebuild.
2. **Does corroboration count as selection?** We dropped `winback_offer_sent`
   and `exit_survey_started` on provenance grounds from the dictionary's
   timing notes, but had already computed their univariate AUCs in A1. Does
   having seen those numbers count as choosing from the data, and does it
   oblige us to use the validation month? Written up in `drafts/a2_draft.md`
   under "On 'no choice was made by looking at data'".
3. **A3's use of the validation month.** We used Sep 2025 to check and then
   correct calibration (Platt). We read that as squarely within "any choice
   made by looking at data", but it is the only fitted correction in Part A
   and is worth one sentence of confirmation.

Related concept settled this session, in case it comes up: cross-validation
carries **two opposing biases**. Fitting on (K-1)/K of the data makes every
candidate's estimate pessimistic for the final refit model (Handout 2 s3.2,
s4.3). Selecting the best of several noisy estimates makes the *winner's*
reported score optimistic (Handout 2 s3.1, "applied one level up"). A
simulation with 25 candidates of identical true risk 400 and CV noise 30
reports 341 for "the best" -- 59 units of pure selection bias with no model
involved. Pessimism is safe, optimism is not, which is why the test set
exists. A2 row 3 compares no candidates, so it carries only the conservative
bias. Both biases will be large in Apparel at n=197 with 25 alphas.

---

## Session 5 — 2026-09-29 (macOS laptop). B1 done. Concepts for B2 settled.

Transcript: `sessions/claude-code/a1-session-5.jsonl` and `.txt`. Same VS Code
thread as sessions 2 and 4, continued, so the file contains those as its
opening parts (see `sessions/README.md` for the containment table).

### What was done

- Confirmed `test_extract("H")` **is** `replicates.csv` filtered to division H
  — verified with `.equals()`. There is no separate test set: one file, two
  jobs (the noise floor, and scoring once). All **eight** B2 cells are scored
  on their own division's 450 replicate rows; nothing is held back.
- `analysis/part2/b1_noise_floor.py`, in `run.sh`. Full run now ~13 s.
- `drafts/b1_draft.md`: exhibit, 62 words of prose, the two-variances note,
  the level-dependence caveat, argue-against list, provenance.
- `drafts/a2_draft.md`: new section on active vs passive use of data, and the
  correction that A2 did not use the validation month but A3 did.
- Office-hours questions recorded above (three, ranked).

### B1 numbers, with provenance (all from `bash analysis/run.sh`)

| Quantity | Value | File |
|---|---|---|
| Hardgoods sigma^2 | 148.11 +/- 10.41 (sigma 12.17 units, mean units 111.88) | `b1_noise_floor.csv` |
| Apparel sigma^2 | 142.04 +/- 9.98 (sigma 11.92 units, mean units 111.04) | " |
| dof | 405 each (45 configs x 9) | " |
| Difference A - H | -6.1, SE 14.4, **0.42 standard errors** | `run.log` |
| Avoidable vs contractor's best | H 82.9 (64% of 231 is floor); A 272.0 (34% of 414 is floor) | `b1_noise_floor.csv` |
| Estimator's assumption | 0 of 12 fields vary within any of the 90 configs; all groups size 10; script raises if not | `b1_config_check.csv` |
| Variance-on-mean slope | H +0.657 +/- 0.410 (consistent with both Poisson and constant); A +0.103 +/- 0.401 (rules out Poisson) | `b1_variance_shape.csv` |
| Overdispersion vs Poisson | 1.32x / 1.28x | " |

**Independent check:** the brief says sigma/sqrt(10) ~ 4 units without naming
a division. Ours give 3.85 and 3.77. That figure was computed by the
instructor from data we had not seen.

**Finding:** the unavoidable error is the same size in both divisions, so the
contractor's "Apparel is inherently harder" does not survive. What differs is
avoidable error: 83 units^2 above the floor in Hardgoods, 272 in Apparel.

### Concepts settled this session (carry into B2/B3)

1. **Two variances, not one.** Handout 3 s1.1's "two levels of randomness".
   **Var** = E_D[(fhat_D(x0) - fbar(x0))^2] is how much the *prediction* moves
   across training samples — reducible. **sigma^2 = Var(eps_0)** is how much
   the *outcome* moves at a fixed x — irreducible. B1 measures the second.
2. **Say "the average unavoidable error".** Handout 3 eq. (4) bars Bias^2 and
   Var but not sigma^2, silently assuming the floor is constant in x. `units`
   is a count and Handout 1 s4.7 warns variance usually grows with the mean.
   Tested: inconclusive (see slopes above). Doesn't threaten the B1
   comparison — both divisions pooled identically over similar demand ranges
   — but would matter for judging a single store-week prediction.
3. **CV carries two opposing biases.** Fitting on (K-1)/K of the data makes
   every candidate's estimate *pessimistic* for the final refit (H2 s3.2,
   s4.3). Selecting the best of several noisy estimates makes the *winner's*
   reported score *optimistic* (H2 s3.1, "applied one level up"). Simulation:
   25 candidates with identical true risk 400 and CV noise 30 report 341 for
   "the best" — 59 units of pure selection bias, no model involved.
   Pessimism is safe; optimism is why the test set exists. A2 row 3 compared
   no candidates, so it carries only the conservative bias.
   **Expect both to be large in Apparel** (n=197, folds fit on ~158 rows, 25
   alphas x 2 degrees), so Apparel's tuned CV number will flatter itself
   relative to its test MSE, and by more than Hardgoods' does.
4. **CV *replaces* a validation set, it does not supplement one** (H2 s3.2 ->
   s4, protocol s6). Part B has no validation set. Part A has a validation
   month only because its holdout is temporal and folds cannot manufacture a
   time-ordered holdout.
5. **The 1-SE rule needs fold-level errors.** H2 s4.5:
   `SE(d) = sd(MSE_1..MSE_K)/sqrt(K)`, then take the *simplest* model whose CV
   error is within one SE of the minimum. Scoring the K fold-models on a
   single shared holdout instead would measure something else — the spread of
   the *predictor* with the evaluation data fixed, which is the **Var** term
   and is what B3 measures with bootstrap resamples. The rule's SE is a
   heuristic: the K training sets overlap heavily so the fold errors are
   correlated, and sd/sqrt(K) treats them as independent.

### B2 — design calls still OPEN (mine to make before coding)

1. **How the penalty is tuned.** (a) tune to the CV minimum and *discuss* the
   1-SE rule in the write-up, or (b) tune *by* the 1-SE rule and report both.
   Whichever is chosen becomes the value frozen into B3's
   `RIDGE_ALPHA_DEG1/DEG2` — `stability.py` says do not re-tune inside the
   resamples. The brief's B2 question 2 says to "note where the
   one-standard-error rule bears on your answer", which reads like (a) plus
   discussion, but (b) is defensible if stated.
2. **Averaging, not pooling — recommended and near-settled.** The brief asks
   for "CV MSE with its standard error across folds", and the SE only exists
   if the five fold-level MSEs are kept. Note A2 *pooled* out-of-fold
   predictions (right for AUC, which is not an average of per-row losses).
   Fold sizes: Hardgoods 600x5 (pooled == averaged), Apparel 40,40,39,39,39
   (they differ slightly). Verified in session 5.
3. **Degree 3?** `DEGREES = (1,2,3)` exists and `basis_size(3)` = 454 terms,
   which against 197 Apparel rows would make the terms-to-rows point vividly.
   The brief specifies four models and says there are no marks for more.
   Recommendation: keep it out of the exhibit; the optional `sweep.py` shows
   it.
4. **Where Part B's figures land** — open since session 1. `sweep.py` writes
   to `sweep_outputs/` and `stability.py` to `b3_outputs/`, both beside the
   script. Session-1 decision: keep the starter's folder names. Remaining
   choice: `analysis/part2/<name>/` vs `analysis/outputs/<name>/`. Everything
   else in this submission writes to `analysis/outputs/`.

### B2 — what the script must do (from the brief's protocol table)

- **Tune:** 5-fold CV on the division's own training extract using
  `make_folds()` (seed 575, do not change). `poly_pipeline(estimator, degree=d)`
  fits the basis and the scaler *inside* each fold. `ALPHA_GRID` is 25
  log-spaced values; the brief warns that if the best value lands on an
  endpoint, something upstream is wrong.
- **Refit** each tuned configuration on all rows of that division's training
  extract. Hardgoods must go through `dedup_h()` (3,350 -> 3,000).
- **Test:** score once on `test_extract("H")` / `test_extract("A")`.
- **Report** per cell: tuned penalty where there is one, CV MSE with its SE
  across folds, test MSE, and **test MSE - sigma^2_hat**.
- Eight cells: {degree 1, degree 2} x {OLS, ridge} x {H, A}.
- Check our degree-1 and degree-2 OLS CV MSEs against the contractor's
  quoted 336 / 231 (H) and 414 / 618 (A). If they land far off, B1's
  "avoidable" column needs restating with our own figures.

### Useful identity for B2's interpretation

All ten replicates of a configuration are identical on every feature, so the
model predicts the same value for all ten and the test MSE splits exactly:

    test MSE = (405/450) * sigma^2_hat + (1/45) * sum_g (ybar_g - yhat_g)^2

The second term is the only part a better model could remove. That is why the
brief insists a raw MSE has no interpretation without the floor beside it.

- B3 after B2 (needs B2's penalties). `stability.py`'s `build_models()` is
  the only thing to fill in; everything else there is fixed.
- Prose budget: A1 499 + A2 552 + A3 448 = 1,499 words for a two-page S1
  (~1,000-1,200). ~450 words to cut; candidates listed at the end of

  `drafts/ai_process_note_draft.md` — that file is a quarry, not a draft, and
  is not submitted.

# Part B — Cascade Outdoor

## The situation

Cascade Outdoor is a specialty retailer with two merchandising divisions: **Hardgoods** (tents, packs, stoves) and **Apparel** (jackets, base layers). Both forecast weekly units per store to drive buying and markdown decisions. Both run on the same planning system and record the same twelve fields.

They differ in size. Apparel migrated to the new platform in March and its pre-migration history is not comparable, so it was dropped. **Hardgoods has about 3,000 usable store-weeks; Apparel has 197.**

A contractor fitted ordinary least squares to each division twice — once on the twelve fields (degree 1, twelve terms) and once on a degree-2 polynomial basis (every square and pairwise product, ninety terms) — and scored both by 5-fold cross-validated MSE:

| | degree 1 | degree 2 |
|---|---:|---:|
| Hardgoods | 336 | **231** |
| Apparel | **414** | 618 |

He drew two conclusions: that the divisions need **different models**, since a different specification wins in each; and that **Apparel demand is inherently harder to forecast**, since its best error of 414 is nearly double Hardgoods' 231.

The VP of Merchandising Planning wants **one modelling standard** company-wide, and has asked you whether that is possible and what it should say.

## What Part B asks

1. How much of the error in each division is unavoidable?
2. What does a penalty buy, and why is the answer different in a division with 3,000 rows than in one with 197?
3. Should Cascade standardise a model, or a process?

There are no marks for a better model than the four specified below.

## What you need to know about the data

Also in `part2_cascade/data/DATA_DICTIONARY.md`.

- **`replicates.csv`** is a controlled exercise: 45 store-week configurations per division, each observed **ten times** with identical values on all twelve recorded fields. None of these rows appear in the training extracts. You use it for two things: estimating the noise floor, and scoring your final models once.
- **Duplicated rows (ticket CO-4471).** The Hardgoods load was re-run and appended rather than replaced, so affected store-weeks appear twice. `dedup_h()` in `load.py` removes them. Use it for everything in Part B.
- **Apparel cleaning.** Three Apparel weeks were removed before delivery (a store closure, a keying error, and Black Friday). The dictionary records the reasoning; you do not need to revisit it.

## The protocol — use exactly this

`part2_cascade/starter/load.py` provides all of it.

| Step | What to do |
|---|---|
| **Tune** | 5-fold CV on the division's own training extract, using `make_folds()`. Fit preprocessing inside each fold — `poly_pipeline(estimator, degree=d)` does this. |
| **Refit** | Refit each tuned configuration on all rows of that division's training extract. |
| **Test** | Score once on `test_extract("H")` or `test_extract("A")`. Nothing is selected by looking at these rows. |
| **Report** | Test MSE, and **test MSE − σ̂²** — the part of the error a better model could in principle remove. |

Standardising before penalising is not optional; `poly_pipeline()` handles it. Handout 3 §5.2 explains why.

## B1 — The noise floor

Each group of ten rows in `replicates.csv` is identical on every recorded field, so the variation within a group is what no model could have predicted. Estimate σ² for each division as the pooled within-configuration variance:

$$\hat{\sigma}^2 = \frac{\sum_g \sum_r (y_{gr} - \bar{y}_g)^2}{\sum_g (m_g - 1)}$$

Report both estimates with their approximate standard error, $\hat{\sigma}^2\sqrt{2/\text{dof}}$, where the degrees of freedom are the denominator of $\hat{\sigma}^2$ above:

$$\text{dof} \;=\; \sum_g (m_g - 1),$$

that is, one fewer than the group size, summed over groups. With 45 configurations of 10 replicates in a division, $\text{dof} = 45 \times 9 = 405$.

Then, in one or two sentences: what do the two floors say about the contractor's claim that Apparel demand is inherently harder to forecast?

## B2 — Four models, compared against the floor

Two degrees crossed with two treatments, in both divisions:

| Model | How to fit it | Tuning |
|---|---|---|
| Degree-1 OLS | `poly_pipeline(LinearRegression(), degree=1)` | none |
| Degree-1 ridge | `poly_pipeline(Ridge(), degree=1)` | `ALPHA_GRID`, 5-fold CV |
| Degree-2 OLS | `poly_pipeline(LinearRegression(), degree=2)` | none |
| Degree-2 ridge | `poly_pipeline(Ridge(), degree=2)` | `ALPHA_GRID`, 5-fold CV |

For each of the eight cells report: the tuned penalty where there is one, CV MSE with its standard error across folds, test MSE, and test MSE − σ̂².

Then answer, in two or three sentences each, citing numbers from your own table:

1. **Where does the penalty change the answer, and where does it not?** Compare the CV MSE with and without a penalty in all four cells. Say what distinguishes the cell where it matters most. (Note that degree-1 Apparel is also a small sample, so "Apparel is small" is not the full explanation — the useful quantity is the number of terms relative to the number of rows.)
2. **Is a single company-wide specification possible?** The contractor compared unpenalised fits only. Once each specification is allowed a tuned penalty, is there one choice that is at least as good as the best hand-picked choice in *both* divisions? Note where the one-standard-error rule (Handout 1 §6.5) bears on your answer.
3. **How firm is the Apparel ordering?** Put the Apparel CV standard errors next to the Hardgoods ones and say which differences in Apparel are large relative to their uncertainty and which are not.

## B3 — One stability experiment

`part2_cascade/starter/stability.py` contains the loop; fill in the four models and interpret the output. Everything else is fixed: **20 bootstrap resamples** from `bootstrap_indices()`, **three query configurations** per division from `QUERY_CONFIGS`, and penalties **held at the B2 values** (do not re-tune inside the resamples).

Each query configuration also appears in `replicates.csv`, so the mean of its ten replicates estimates the true mean demand there to within about σ/√10 ≈ 4 units. That is what lets you see bias and variance separately rather than only their sum.

For each division and model, report at each query point:

- the **standard deviation** of the 20 predictions (variance), and
- the **difference between their mean and the replicate-based estimate of the truth** (bias).

Report the average of each across the three points, plus one figure.

Then, in one sentence each, backed by a number from your table:

1. What raising the degree from 1 to 2 did to bias and to variance, in each division.
2. What the penalty did to each, at each degree.
3. Why the same four procedures give different answers in the two divisions. Name the quantity that differs and connect it to B2.

If one of these effects is nearly zero, say so and give the number.

**Optional, no marks, about two minutes.** `part2_cascade/starter/sweep.py` plots training and cross-validated error against the number of terms in the basis, and cross-validated error against the ridge penalty, for both divisions. Running it is the fastest way to see the curves in Handout 3 §1.4, and Midterm 1 will ask you to read a plot like them.

[tool_result]
"""
Assignment 1, Part B starter -- Cascade Outdoor weekly unit forecasting.

Everything that would otherwise differ between submissions for no good reason
is fixed here: the fold assignments, the polynomial bases, the penalty grids,
the bootstrap resamples and the query points.

Nothing here cleans the data, estimates the floor, fits a model, chooses a
penalty or interprets anything.  Those are the assignment.

Usage:
    from load import (load_h, load_a, load_replicates, test_extract,
                      dedup_h, FEATURES, TARGET, make_folds,
                      poly_pipeline, DEGREES, ALPHA_GRID,
                      bootstrap_indices, QUERY_CONFIGS, query_frame,
                      N_BOOTSTRAP)
"""
from pathlib import Path

import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from sklearn.model_selection import KFold
from sklearn.pipeline import Pipeline
from sklearn.preprocessing import PolynomialFeatures, StandardScaler

DATA = Path(__file__).resolve().parents[1] / "data"

TARGET = "units"
ID_COLS = ("store_id", "week_id")

# The twelve recorded predictor fields, in extract order.  Both divisions
# record all twelve.  Whether all twelve matter is not stated anywhere.
FEATURES = [
    "promo_depth_pct", "own_price_index", "competitor_price_index",
    "marketing_spend_k", "temp_f", "loyalty_share", "store_sqft_k",
    "inventory_cover_wks", "assortment_breadth", "catalog_pages",
    "returns_rate", "staffing_hours",
]

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- loaders

def load_h() -> pd.DataFrame:
    """Hardgoods store-weeks, exactly as the pipeline delivered them.

    Read the pipeline note in the data dictionary before you model on this.
    """
    return pd.read_csv(DATA / "division_h.csv")


def load_a() -> pd.DataFrame:
    """Apparel store-weeks.  Already cleaned -- see the data dictionary."""
    return pd.read_csv(DATA / "division_a.csv")


def load_replicates() -> pd.DataFrame:
    """The controlled repeat-observation extract, and the final test set.

    Use it for two things and no others: estimating the noise floor, and
    scoring each tuned model exactly once at the end.
    """
    return pd.read_csv(DATA / "replicates.csv")


def test_extract(division: str) -> pd.DataFrame:
    """The replicate rows for one division ('H' or 'A').  Your test set.

    Score each tuned configuration on this exactly once, at the end.
    """
    r = load_replicates()
    return r.loc[r["division"] == division].reset_index(drop=True)


def dedup_h(df: pd.DataFrame) -> pd.DataFrame:
    """The Hardgoods extract with the duplicated block removed.

    The load behind `division_h.csv` was re-run and appended rather than
    replaced (ticket CO-4471), so affected store-weeks appear twice.  Use
    this for everything in Part B.
    """
    return df.drop_duplicates(subset=["week_id"]).reset_index(drop=True)


# ------------------------------------------------- the fixed CV protocol

CV_SEED = 575


def make_folds(n_splits: int = 5) -> KFold:
    """The fold assignment every submission uses.  Do not change the seed."""
    return KFold(n_splits=n_splits, shuffle=True, random_state=CV_SEED)


# The flexibility dial for this assignment.  Degree 1 is the twelve recorded
# fields as they come; degree 2 adds every square and every pairwise product;
# degree 3 adds every triple.  The term counts are worth knowing before you
# fit anything, and `basis_size()` prints them.
DEGREES = (1, 2, 3)


def basis_size(degree: int, p: int = len(FEATURES)) -> int:
    """Number of terms in the degree-`degree` basis on `p` inputs."""
    from math import comb
    return comb(p + degree, degree) - 1


def poly_pipeline(estimator, degree: int = 2) -> Pipeline:
    """Degree-`degree` basis -> standardise -> estimator, as one pipeline.

    Fitting this inside a cross-validation fold fits the expansion and the
    scaler on the training part of that fold only, which is the point.
    Passing the estimator a pre-expanded, pre-scaled matrix would leak the
    held-out rows into the preprocessing; see Handout 1 §7.2.

    Standardising *before* the penalty is not optional.  Ridge penalises
    coefficients, so it penalises a term measured in thousands far less than
    the same term measured in units; on a raw polynomial basis the result is
    approximately OLS wearing a penalty's name.  Handout 3 §5.2.
    """
    return Pipeline([
        ("poly", PolynomialFeatures(degree=degree, include_bias=False)),
        ("scale", StandardScaler()),
        ("est", estimator),
    ])


# 25 penalties, log-spaced.  Both divisions' best value falls inside this
# range at every degree; if yours lands on an endpoint, something upstream
# is wrong.
ALPHA_GRID = np.logspace(-2, 4, 25)


# ------------------------------------------------------- Part B3 fixtures

N_BOOTSTRAP = 20

# Three configurations per division -- low, middle and high demand.  Each one
# also appears in replicates.csv, so the average of its ten replicate weeks is
# an estimate of the true mean demand there, accurate to about sigma/sqrt(10).
QUERY_CONFIGS = {
    "H": ["H43", "H16", "H01"],
    "A": ["A10", "A08", "A07"],
}


def query_frame(division: str) -> pd.DataFrame:
    """The three query points for a division, one row each, FEATURES columns.

    Indexed by config_id and in the order given by QUERY_CONFIGS.
    """
    r = load_replicates()
    r = r.loc[r["division"] == division]
    first = r.groupby("config_id")[FEATURES].first()
    return first.loc[QUERY_CONFIGS[division]]


def bootstrap_indices(n: int, n_draws: int = N_BOOTSTRAP, seed: int = CV_SEED):
    """Yield `n_draws` index arrays, each a bootstrap resample of size n.

    Fixed so that everyone's B3 resamples the same way and differences
    between submissions come from the modelling, not the plumbing.
    """
    rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)
    for _ in range(n_draws):
        yield rng.integers(0, n, n)


# ------------------------------------------------------------------ sanity

if __name__ == "__main__":
    for name, df in (("division_h", load_h()), ("division_a", load_a())):
        print(f"{name:12s} rows={len(df):5,}  features={len(FEATURES)}  "
              f"units: mean={df[TARGET].mean():7.1f} sd={df[TARGET].std():6.1f} "
              f"min={df[TARGET].min():7.1f} max={df[TARGET].max():8.1f}")
    r = load_replicates()
    print(f"replicates   rows={len(r):5,}  configs={r['config_id'].nunique()}  "
          f"divisions={sorted(r['division'].unique())}  "
          f"rows per config={sorted(r.groupby('config_id').size().unique())}")
    print()
    print("terms in each basis, on 12 inputs:")
    for d in DEGREES:
        print(f"  degree {d}: {basis_size(d):5,} terms")
    for d in ("H", "A"):
        print(f"query points {d}: {QUERY_CONFIGS[d]}")

# B1 draft — the noise floor

Draft for the B1 part of memo §2 (to the VP of Merchandising Planning). Every
number is in `analysis/outputs/` from `bash analysis/run.sh` (session 5,
2026-09-29).

**Prose budget.** §2 is one page for B1, B2 and B3 together — roughly 450–500
words. The brief asks B1 for "one or two sentences," so B1's share is about
60 words. Everything else on this page is notes, not memo text.

---

## Exhibit B1 — How much of the error is unavoidable

Source: `analysis/outputs/b1_noise_floor.csv`, `b1_config_check.csv`.
Pooled within-configuration variance over 45 configurations of 10 replicates
per division, so dof = 45 × 9 = 405 in each.

| Division | σ̂² | Approx. SE | σ̂ (units) | Mean units |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| Hardgoods | **148.1** | ± 10.4 | 12.2 | 111.9 |
| Apparel | **142.0** | ± 10.0 | 11.9 | 111.0 |

Difference (Apparel − Hardgoods) = −6.1, standard error 14.4: **0.42 standard
errors, indistinguishable from zero.**

Against the contractor's own cross-validated figures:

| Division | His best CV MSE | Floor σ̂² | Avoidable | Share that is floor |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| Hardgoods | 231 (degree 2) | 148.1 | **82.9** | 64% |
| Apparel | 414 (degree 1) | 142.0 | **272.0** | 34% |

*Footnote for the exhibit:* the estimator assumes the twelve recorded fields
are constant inside a configuration. Checked: 0 of 12 fields vary within any
of the 90 configurations, every group has exactly 10 rows, and `units` varies
within every group. The script raises rather than computing a floor if that
fails.

---

## Prose (draft, 62 words)

The unavoidable error is the same size in both divisions: 148 units² in
Hardgoods and 142 in Apparel, a difference of 0.4 standard errors. Apparel
demand is therefore not inherently harder to forecast. What differs is how
much error is left on the table — 83 units² above the floor in Hardgoods
against 272 in Apparel, so roughly two-thirds of Hardgoods' error is
irreducible and only one-third of Apparel's is.

---

## Notes to self (not memo text)

### The two variances, and why only one of them is this number

Handout 3 §1.1 lists "two levels of randomness": the noise ε₀ in the new case,
and the training set 𝒟 and hence the predictor built from it. Equation (2)
keeps them apart —

  Err(x₀) = Bias² + **Var** + **σ²**

where **Var** = E_𝒟[(f̂_𝒟(x₀) − f̄(x₀))²] is how much the *prediction* moves
across training samples (reducible: more data, or a penalty, shrinks it), and
**σ² = Var(ε₀)** is how much the *outcome* moves at a fixed x (irreducible).
B1 measures the second: ten weeks identical on every recorded field, and
`units` still differs. The dartboard in §2.3 — "how spread out the cloud is"
versus "the scatter in the target itself."

### Say "the average irreducible error", not "the irreducible error"

Strictly the floor is Var(ε | X = x), which may depend on where you are in
feature space. Handout 3 §2.2 writes the population version as
E[Err] = Bias²̄ + Var̄ + σ², with bars on the first two terms and none on σ² —
which silently assumes the floor is the same everywhere. `units` is a count,
and Handout 1 §4.7 warns that for counts "the variance typically grows with
the mean," so variance–mean coupling is the default expectation here, not an
exotic worry.

Tested it (`b1_variance_shape.csv`): regressing a configuration's variance on its
mean gives a slope of **+0.657 ± 0.410** in Hardgoods and **+0.103 ± 0.401**
in Apparel. Poisson would be slope 1, constant variance slope 0. Hardgoods is
consistent with *both* — the experiment cannot resolve it, because a single
configuration's variance is estimated from ten observations and carries a
standard error near 70 on a value of 148. Both divisions are mildly
overdispersed relative to Poisson (σ̂² ≈ 1.3 × mean).

Consequences:
- It does **not** threaten the B1 comparison. Both divisions were pooled
  identically, over configurations spanning similar demand ranges
  (Hardgoods 51–176 units, Apparel 80–182), so we are comparing two averages
  computed the same way.
- It **would** matter if σ̂² were used to judge a single prediction at a
  specific store-week: the floor there could be above or below 148.
- So the memo says "the average unavoidable error", and B2's "test MSE − σ̂²"
  is a distance from an average floor, not from a pointwise one.

### An independent check that the arithmetic is right

The brief states, in the B3 section, that a configuration's replicate mean
estimates true demand "to within about σ/√10 ≈ 4 units", without saying which
division. Ours give **3.85** (Hardgoods) and **3.77** (Apparel). That number
was computed by the instructor from data we had not seen, and our estimate
lands on it. Worth one line in the process note.

### Why "test MSE − σ̂²" is the right thing to report (carry into B2)

All ten replicates of a configuration are identical on every feature, so the
model predicts the same value for all ten. The test MSE therefore splits
exactly:

  test MSE = (405/450)·σ̂² + (1/45)·Σ_g (ȳ_g − ŷ_g)²

The second term is the only part a better model could remove — the squared
error of the model against the *true* configuration means. Subtracting σ̂² is
approximately recovering it. Useful to be able to say out loud, and it
explains why the brief calls a raw MSE uninterpretable.

### Arguing against the draft

- **"Identical" is too strong.** A 95% interval on the difference of the two
  floors is roughly ±28 units², up to about 20% of a floor. The defensible
  phrasing is "indistinguishable at this precision", which is still far more
  than enough to sink 414-versus-231.
- **The 45 configurations are not a random sample.** They are points
  Merchandising Analytics chose during the 2025 platform validation. Nothing
  in the dictionary claims they are representative of where the training rows
  live, so the floor is estimated where they looked.
- **The contractor's 231 and 414 are quoted, not reproduced.** B2 refits those
  specifications under the prescribed protocol; if our degree-1 and degree-2
  OLS numbers land far from his, the "avoidable" column above needs restating
  with our own figures. Check this when B2 runs.
- **Same floor does not mean same business difficulty.** Apparel still has 197
  usable rows, and the dictionary says parity is not expected until late 2027.
  "Not inherently harder" is a claim about demand, not about Apparel's
  forecasting problem being easy.

### Numbers used and where they live

| Number | File |
|---|---|
| σ̂² 148.1 ± 10.4, σ̂ 12.2, mean units 111.9 (Hardgoods) | `b1_noise_floor.csv` |
| σ̂² 142.0 ± 10.0, σ̂ 11.9, mean units 111.0 (Apparel) | " |
| dof 405, 45 configs × 10 replicates, both divisions | " |
| Avoidable 82.9 / 272.0; floor share 64% / 34% | " |
| 0 of 12 fields vary within a config; all groups size 10 | `b1_config_check.csv` |
| Difference −6.1, SE 14.4, 0.42 SE | `run.log` |
| Contractor's 336 / 231 / 414 / 618 | quoted from the brief, not computed |
| Variance-on-mean slopes +0.657 ± 0.410, +0.103 ± 0.401; overdispersion 1.32× / 1.28×; config mean ranges 51–176 / 80–182 | `b1_variance_shape.csv` |

Deadline Friday midnight and office hours Friday 11:00 is good news: the three questions get answered before you submit, and the A2 "building data" question is cheap to act on afterwards.

## 1. `ALPHA_GRID`, and one correction

`ALPHA_GRID = np.logspace(-2, 4, 25)`: **25 values, log-spaced from 0.01 to 10,000**, each step about 1.78×. Ridge minimises squared error plus α × (sum of squared coefficients), so α is the price of coefficient size. α = 0.01 is almost OLS; α = 10,000 crushes the coefficients toward zero. Log spacing because what matters is the order of magnitude, not the absolute step.

**The correction:** the grid is scored by **cross-validated MSE on the training extract**, not test MSE. You said "choose the min test MSE one out of all of them." That would be selecting on the test set, which the brief forbids and which would make the test number meaningless. The loop is: for each of the 25 α, run 5-fold CV on the training rows, average the five fold MSEs, pick the α with the lowest average. The test extract is touched once, after refitting on all training rows. The brief's endpoint warning is a sanity check: if the winner is α = 0.01 or α = 10,000, the true optimum is outside the grid and something upstream is wrong.

**On "tuning by 1-SE would make the 1-SE choice biased":** that is not quite the issue. The 1-SE rule is not an attempt at an unbiased estimate; it is a deliberate preference for the simpler model, on the grounds that the CV minimum is itself noisy and the dip at the minimum is often smaller than the uncertainty around it. You trade a little CV error for a model that moves less when the data change. Nothing about it is dishonest, and measuring it on the test set afterwards is just as valid.

The real reasons to prefer the CV minimum here are the ones you can defend: the brief's question 2 says to *note where the rule bears on the answer*, which asks for discussion; one criterion across all four cells keeps the eight cells comparable; and the rule then does real work in question 2, where "is one specification good enough in both divisions" is exactly the question the rule is designed to answer. Your conclusion is right, so I will tune to the CV minimum and report the 1-SE choice alongside as a diagnostic, which costs nothing since the fold errors are already computed.

## 2. Pooling versus averaging

Two ways to turn five folds into one number:

- **Averaging:** compute MSE inside fold 1, fold 2, … fold 5, then average the five. You get five numbers, so you get a standard error.
- **Pooling:** collect every out-of-fold prediction into one vector of length n and compute a single MSE over all n rows. You get one number and no standard error.

With equal fold sizes they are identical. With Apparel's 40/40/39/39/39 they differ slightly, because pooling weights folds by size and averaging does not. A2 pooled, correctly, because AUC is not an average of per-row losses and cannot be fold-averaged meaningfully. Here the brief asks for the SE across folds, so averaging is the only option that produces what is asked. Your understanding was right; I raised it only because A2 did the opposite and the inconsistency would look like an error if nobody explained it.

## 3 and 4

Agreed, no degree 3. Agreed, `analysis/outputs/`, no reason to break the norm.

Writing `b2_models.py` now.

All ten replicates of a\n                # configuration are identical on every feature, so the model\n                # predicts one value for the group and the MSE separates into\n                # the within-group scatter (the floor) and the squared error\n                # against the group means (what a better model could remove).\n                g = test.groupby(\"config_id\")\n                ybar = g[TARGET].mean()\n                yhat = pd.Series(pred, index=test.index).groupby(test[\"config_id\"]).first()\n                within_ss = float(((test[TARGET] - test[\"config_id\"].map(ybar)) ** 2).sum())\n                struct = float(((ybar - yhat) ** 2).mean())\n                decomp.append({\n                    \"division\": DIVISION_NAME[div], \"degree\": degree,\n                    \"treatment\": treatment, \"test_mse\": round(test_mse, 2),\n                    \"floor_part\": round(within_ss / len(test), 2),\n                    \"structural_part\": round(struct, 2),\n                    \"identity_check\": round(within_ss / len(test) + struct - test_mse, 10),\n                    \"test_mse_minus_sigma2\": round(test_mse - sigma2[div], 2),\n                })\n        print()\n\n    table = pd.DataFrame(rows)\n    table.to_csv(OUT / \"b2_table.csv\", index=False)\n    pd.concat(curves, ignore_index=True).to_csv(OUT / \"b2_alpha_curves.csv\", index=False)\n    table.loc[table[\"treatment\"].eq(\"ridge\"),\n              [\"division\", \"degree\", \"tuned_alpha\", \"alpha_1se\"]] \\\n         .to_csv(OUT / \"b2_penalties.csv\", index=False)\n    pd.DataFrame(decomp).to_csv(OUT / \"b2_test_decomposition.csv\", index=False)\n\n    # Our OLS CV MSEs against the contractor's quoted figures.\n    check = []\n    for (div, degree), quoted in CONTRACTOR_CV.items():\n        m = table.query(\"division == @DIVISION_NAME[@div] and degree == @degree \"\n                        \"and treatment == 'OLS'\").iloc[0]\n        check.append({\"division\": DIVISION_NAME[div], \"degree\": degree,\n                      \"contractor_quoted\": quoted, \"ours_cv_mse\": m[\"cv_mse\"],\n                      \"ours_cv_se\": m[\"cv_se\"],\n                      \"difference\": round(m[\"cv_mse\"] - quoted, 2),\n                      \"ses_from_quoted\": round((m[\"cv_mse\"] - quoted) / m[\"cv_se\"], 2)})\n    check = pd.DataFrame(check)\n    check.to_csv(OUT / \"b2_contractor_check.csv\", index=False)\n\n    # Every pairwise CV difference inside a division, paired across the five\n    # shared folds.  Pairing is the right comparison: the folds are identical\n    # across cells, so the fold-to-fold variation cancels.\n    diffs = []\n    for div in DIVISIONS:\n        cells = [c for c in folds_by_cell if c.startswith(DIVISION_NAME[div])]\n        for i, a in enumerate(cells):\n            for b in cells[i + 1:]:\n                d = folds_by_cell[a] - folds_by_cell[b]\n                mean, se = cv_summary(d)\n                diffs.append({\"division\": DIVISION_NAME[div],\n                              \"model_a\": a.replace(DIVISION_NAME[div] + \" \", \"\"),\n                              \"model_b\": b.replace(DIVISION_NAME[div] + \" \", \"\"),\n                              \"mean_difference\": round(mean, 2),\n                              \"se_of_difference\": round(se, 2),\n                              \"ses_from_zero\": round(mean / se, 2) if se else np.nan})\n    diffs = pd.DataFrame(diffs)\n    diffs.to_csv(OUT / \"b2_differences.csv\", index=False)\n\n    print(\"B2 exhibit\")\n    print(table[[\"division\", \"degree\", \"treatment\", \"terms\", \"terms_per_row\",\n                 \"tuned_alpha\", \"cv_mse\", \"cv_se\", \"test_mse\",\n                 \"test_mse_minus_sigma2\"]].to_string(index=False))\n    print()\n    print(\"tuned penalty vs the one-standard-error choice (diagnostic)\")\n    print(table.loc[table[\"treatment\"].eq(\"ridge\"),\n                    [\"division\", \"degree\", \"tuned_alpha\", \"cv_mse\", \"cv_se\",\n                     \"alpha_1se\", \"cv_mse_1se\", \"alpha_on_grid_endpoint\"]]\n               .to_string(index=False))\n    print()\n    print(\"our OLS CV MSE against the contractor's quoted figures\")\n    print(check.to_string(index=False))\n    print()\n    print(\"pairwise CV differences, paired across the five shared folds\")\n    print(diffs.to_string(index=False))\n    print()\n    print(\"exact split of each test MSE (floor part + structural part)\")\n    print(pd.DataFrame(decomp).to_string(index=False))\n    print()\n    print(f\"wrote {OUT / 'b2_table.csv'}, b2_alpha_curves.csv, b2_penalties.csv, \"\n          f\"b2_contractor_check.csv, b2_differences.csv, b2_test_decomposition.csv\")\n\n\nif __name__ == \"__main__\":\n    main()\n"
}
