B2 runs inside `run.sh`; the whole pipeline is 16 seconds. Results, then the three questions.

## Exhibit B2

| Division | Degree | Treatment | Terms | Terms/row | α | CV MSE | ± SE | Test MSE | Test − σ̂² |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| Hardgoods | 1 | OLS | 12 | 0.004 | | 336.62 | 6.42 | 398.55 | 250.44 |
| Hardgoods | 1 | ridge | 12 | 0.004 | 10.0 | 336.61 | 6.39 | 398.38 | 250.27 |
| Hardgoods | 2 | OLS | 90 | 0.030 | | 233.12 | 4.64 | 246.16 | 98.05 |
| Hardgoods | 2 | ridge | 90 | 0.030 | 0.316 | **232.98** | 4.79 | 247.47 | 99.36 |
| Apparel | 1 | OLS | 12 | 0.061 | | 413.53 | 29.84 | 363.58 | 221.54 |
| Apparel | 1 | ridge | 12 | 0.061 | 10.0 | 412.06 | 36.19 | 354.12 | 212.08 |
| Apparel | 2 | OLS | 90 | 0.457 | | 617.97 | 67.54 | 366.86 | 224.82 |
| Apparel | 2 | ridge | 90 | 0.457 | 3.162 | **329.80** | 14.31 | 296.19 | 154.15 |

No tuned α sits on a grid endpoint.

**The contractor's numbers reproduce almost exactly.** Ours against his: 336.62 vs 336, 233.12 vs 231, 413.53 vs 414, 617.97 vs 618. All within half a standard error. So B1's "avoidable" column stands as drafted, and his arithmetic was never the problem; his conclusions were.

## What the numbers say about the three questions

**1. Where the penalty matters.** One cell, overwhelmingly: Apparel degree 2, where CV MSE falls from 617.97 to 329.80. Paired across the shared folds that is 288.16 ± 55.02, over five standard errors. The other three cells are nothing: Hardgoods degree 1 is 0.01 ± 0.05, Hardgoods degree 2 is 0.15 ± 0.22, Apparel degree 1 is 1.47 ± 7.26, none as much as one standard error. The distinguishing quantity is terms per row, and it separates the cells cleanly: 0.457 where the penalty matters against 0.004, 0.030 and 0.061 where it does not. Apparel degree 1 is the cell that kills the "Apparel is small" explanation: same 197 rows, 12 terms instead of 90, and the penalty buys nothing.

**2. A single company-wide specification is possible, and it is degree-2 ridge.** The contractor compared unpenalised fits and concluded the divisions need different models. Once each specification gets a tuned penalty that reverses: degree-2 ridge is 232.98 in Hardgoods against the best hand-picked 233.12, a difference of 0.15 ± 0.22, and 329.80 in Apparel against the best hand-picked 413.53, better by 83.72 ± 19.98. So it ties the winner in one division and beats it in the other.

The 1-SE rule bears on it twice. In Hardgoods it is why standardising costs nothing: degree-2 ridge and degree-2 OLS are well inside one standard error of each other, so there is no evidence to prefer the one the contractor picked. And the rule explains why the exact α barely matters: 12 of the 25 Hardgoods degree-2 α values are within one SE of the minimum, and 19 of 25 at degree 1. The penalty is doing almost nothing there and any α in a wide band would do.

**3. The Apparel ordering is firm at the top, not at the bottom.** Apparel's CV standard errors run 14 to 68 against Hardgoods' 4.6 to 6.4, three to ten times larger on 197 rows against 3,000. Firm: degree-2 ridge beats both degree-1 cells by 82 to 84 with SEs of 20 to 26, three to four standard errors. Not firm: degree-1 OLS versus degree-1 ridge, 1.47 ± 7.26. And degree-2 OLS is firmly the worst, 204.44 ± 52.90 behind degree-1 OLS.

## Two things worth your judgment before I draft

**The subtraction the brief asks for is slightly off, and I can show by how much.** Because all ten replicates of a configuration share their features, the test MSE splits exactly into (405/450)·σ̂² plus the squared error against the configuration means. The identity holds to zero in every cell. The consequence: the floor occupies only 90% of the test MSE, so "test MSE − σ̂²" **understates** the removable error by exactly σ̂²/10, about 14.8 in Hardgoods and 14.2 in Apparel. Hardgoods degree-2 OLS, for instance, reports 98.05 but the removable part is 112.86. It is in `b2_test_decomposition.csv`. I would report the brief's quantity as asked and add one footnote; your call whether that is worth the words in a one-page section.

**CV and test disagree in opposite directions in the two divisions.** Apparel's test MSE comes in *below* its CV MSE (363.58 against 413.53 at degree 1), which is the pessimism bias your session-5 notes predicted: folds fit on 158 rows while the refit gets all 197. Hardgoods degree 1 goes the other way, test 398.55 against CV 336.62, which points at the 45 chosen configurations not being representative of where the training rows live, the caveat already in the B1 draft. Both are honest and worth a line, but they are also the kind of thing that eats a one-page budget.

Tell me how much of those two you want in §2, and whether you agree with my reading of the three answers, and I will draft B2. Anything you disagree with, say it now rather than after the draft exists.

Four things, and one of them is a correction to what I told you earlier.

## 1. Tuning by the CV minimum: my stated reason was wrong

First, to clarify the setup. There is **no validation set anywhere in Part B**. Cross-validation replaces one; it does not supplement one (your session-5 note 4). So for every cell, the CV MSE is the average of the five out-of-fold MSEs on the training extract, and the only honest holdout is the replicate extract, scored once at the end.

Now the correction. I implied tuning by the CV minimum keeps the ridge cells and the OLS cells comparable. It does not, and the asymmetry runs the other way:

- The **OLS cells** involve no selection. Their CV MSE is one estimate, and it carries only the conservative bias (folds fit on 4/5 of the rows).
- The **ridge cells** report the *minimum of 25 noisy estimates*. That is the winner's curse from your session-5 note 3, applied one level up. The reported number is optimistically biased, and the bias is larger the flatter and noisier the curve.

So comparing a ridge CV MSE against an OLS CV MSE slightly flatters ridge. The honest moves are to disclose it and to let the test column arbitrate, and the evidence is already sitting in the table: in Hardgoods degree 2, ridge beats OLS by 0.15 in CV and *loses* to it by 1.31 on test. That is exactly what a small selection bias looks like.

This does not change any conclusion, and it sharpens two:

- The Apparel degree-2 gain of 288.16 ± 55.02 is far too large for selection bias to explain. It stands.
- The Hardgoods ties become *more* convincingly ties. If ridge's CV number is if anything flattered and it still only matches OLS, then there is genuinely nothing to choose between them there, which is what question 2 needs.

The real reasons to tune by the CV minimum remain: the brief asks you to *discuss* the 1-SE rule rather than tune by it, one criterion across eight cells is simpler to defend, and it is the standard choice. Your conclusion holds; my justification for it was the wrong one.

## 2. Why A2 pooled, and a second correction

I said AUC "cannot be fold-averaged meaningfully." Too strong. It can be averaged, and often is. Here is the accurate version.

**MSE averages because it is already an average of per-row losses.** Each row contributes its own (y − ŷ)², so a fold's MSE is the mean of its rows' losses and the average of the five folds is a mean of means. The quantity is defined one row at a time.

**AUC is not defined one row at a time.** It is the probability that a randomly chosen churner outranks a randomly chosen non-churner, so it is computed over *pairs*. In A2's building window that is 1,767 churners × 19,264 non-churners, about 34 million pairs.

- **Pooling** puts every out-of-fold score in one vector and uses all 34 million pairs, including pairs whose two members were scored by *different* fold models.
- **Averaging** computes AUC inside each fold and averages, which uses only within-fold pairs, about a fifth of them.

Both are legitimate and they answer slightly different questions. Pooling asks "how well does this procedure rank the whole population," which is the deployment question, since in April 2026 one model ranks one book and one threshold is applied to all of it. Averaging asks "how well does it rank within a fifth of the book."

The decisive reason in A2 was narrower: `analyst_model.py` pools (it fills an `oof` array and calls `roc_auc_score` once on it), and row 0 had to reproduce the analyst's protocol exactly. Switching to averaging would have changed the protocol and broken that. And A2 was never asked for a standard error, while B2 explicitly is, which is why the two parts differ.

## 3. The test MSE split, high level then the one line of algebra

**High level: it is Pythagoras.** Pick one configuration. All ten of its weeks have identical features, so the model outputs a single number ŷ for all ten. Now ask how far each week's actual units sits from that prediction, and route the distance through the group's own average ȳ:

```
       week's units  ────────────►  prediction
            │  (how far this week    ▲
            │   is from its group's  │  (how far the group's
            ▼   own average)         │   average is from ŷ)
       group average ────────────────┘
```

The first leg is noise: ten identical weeks and the units still differ, and no model could have known. The second leg is the model's miss: it is predicting the wrong level for this configuration. The two legs are perpendicular, so the squared distances add with no cross-term.

**Why perpendicular, in one line.** Write y − ŷ as (y − ȳ) + (ȳ − ŷ) and square it. The cross-term is 2(ȳ − ŷ)·Σ(y − ȳ), and **Σ(y − ȳ) = 0** because deviations from their own mean always sum to zero. That is the whole trick. So per configuration:

```
Σ(y − ŷ)² = Σ(y − ȳ)²  +  10·(ȳ − ŷ)²
             noise          model's miss
```

**Now the 405/450.** Sum over the 45 configurations and divide by 450 rows:

- The noise part becomes Σ(all within-group squared deviations)/450. But that numerator *is* 405·σ̂², because σ̂² was defined as exactly that sum divided by 405. So the noise part is **(405/450)·σ̂² = 0.9·σ̂²**.
- The miss part becomes (10/450)·Σ(ȳ − ŷ)² = **the average of (ȳ − ŷ)² over the 45 configurations**.

The 405 versus 450 is just the n−1 in a variance: σ̂² divides by 405 degrees of freedom, the MSE divides by 450 rows, and 405/450 = 0.9.

**The consequence.** The floor is only 90% of the test MSE, so subtracting a full σ̂² takes out too much:

```
test MSE − σ̂²  =  (model's miss)  −  σ̂²/10
```

σ̂²/10 is 14.8 in Hardgoods and 14.2 in Apparel. Hardgoods degree-2 OLS: the brief's quantity is 98.05, and the actual removable error is 112.86. The identity checks to zero in all eight cells, and the floor part comes out at 133.30 and 127.83, which are 0.9 × 148.11 and 0.9 × 142.04.

Note what this says about the brief's own instruction: "test MSE − σ̂²" is the right *idea* and is conservative, understating what a better model could win by a fixed 14.8 or 14.2. It is only exact because these ten-replicate groups let us compute the thing it approximates.

## 4. CV versus test divergence

Agreed, it goes in. It is the one place in Part B where two separate course ideas show up in the same two numbers: training-set size (Apparel's folds fit on 158 rows while the refit gets 197, so CV is pessimistic and the test comes in lower) and sample representativeness (Hardgoods degree 1 goes the other way, test above CV, because the 45 chosen configurations are not a random sample of where the training rows live).

---

Nothing here needs new code. Before I draft B2, one question that is yours: the selection-bias point in item 1 is a real caveat on the ridge CV numbers, and it is not in the brief's three questions. Does it go in §2 as a clause, or into the notes and the process note only? §2 is one page for B1 to B3, so it costs words that question 3 might want.

Forget the decomposition for a moment. Ask a simpler question.

## What would a perfect model score?

Imagine the best model that could possibly exist. It cannot know which of the ten identical weeks is which, so the best it can do is predict each configuration's **true mean demand**. Suppose it even knows that mean exactly. What test MSE does it get?

Not σ̂². It gets **133.30** in Hardgoods, against σ̂² = 148.11. I just computed it: predict each configuration's own replicate mean and score it. Exactly 0.9 × σ̂², in both divisions.

So:

```
perfect model's test MSE − σ̂²  =  133.30 − 148.11  =  −14.81
```

A flawless model scores **minus 14.81** on the brief's "distance above the floor." Not zero. Every model's number is shifted down by the same 14.81, which is why the quantity understates the removable error by that much.

## Why a perfect model does not score σ̂²

Because σ̂² and "average squared error" divide the same numerator by different denominators.

Take three numbers: 8, 10, 12. Their mean is 10, and the squared deviations are 4, 0, 4, summing to **8**. That 8 is the numerator for both quantities. Now:

| Quantity | Denominator | Result |
|---|---|---|
| Variance estimate σ̂² | n − 1 = **2** | 8 / 2 = **4.00** |
| Average squared error of a model predicting 10 | n = **3** | 8 / 3 = **2.67** |

Same 8 on top. The variance divides by 2, the MSE divides by 3. So the MSE of a perfect model is (n−1)/n of the variance, here 2/3.

With ten replicates instead of three, (n−1)/n = 9/10 = 0.9. Across 45 configurations that is 405/450. That is the entire source of the 0.9.

## Why variance divides by n − 1 at all

Because you estimated the mean from the same ten numbers you are measuring deviations from. The sample mean sits, by construction, in the middle of your ten numbers, closer to them than the true mean would be. So the squared deviations you observe are a bit too small, and dividing by 9 instead of 10 inflates them back to compensate. That is Bessel's correction, and it is the right thing to do when you want to estimate the *true* variance.

But a model's MSE is not estimating anything. It is a plain average: add up the squared misses, divide by how many rows there were. 450 rows, divide by 450.

So the two numbers answer different questions:

- **σ̂² = 148.11** is "what is the true week-to-week variance at a fixed configuration?"
- **133.30** is "what does a perfect model actually score on these 450 rows?"

The brief asks you to subtract the first from the test MSE, but the first is 11% larger than what a perfect model would score, so the subtraction overshoots by σ̂²/10.

## Is the brief wrong?

No, and this is worth saying in the memo in one clause. σ̂² is the right thing to subtract if the question is "how far is this model from the true noise floor of the process." It is conservative: it makes every model look 14.81 closer to perfect than it is. The exact version is available to us only because these groups have ten replicates each, which lets us compute what the subtraction approximates instead of approximating it.

Does that land? If so, tell me whether the §2 footnote should state it as "the brief's quantity is conservative by σ̂²/10, about 15 units²" or spell out the perfect-model framing, and I will draft B2 with your three answers, the OLS-versus-ridge comparability clause, and the CV-versus-test divergence.

## The extreme case, where it is impossible to miss

Suppose a configuration had only **two** identical weeks instead of ten, and they came in at 8 and 12 units. The true mean demand is 10.

Both quantities start from the same sum: deviations from the mean are −2 and +2, squared they are 4 and 4, **sum = 8**.

- **σ̂²** = 8 / (n − 1) = 8 / **1** = **8**
- **A perfect model** predicts 10. Its errors are −2 and +2, squared 4 and 4, averaged over 2 rows = 8 / **2** = **4**

σ̂² is **double** what the perfect model scores. Subtract and you get 4 − 8 = **−4**, which is half the floor, from a model that is doing everything right.

Why the two differ: σ̂² is divided by 1 because you spent one of your two observations working out where the mean was. The perfect model's MSE is divided by 2 because there were two rows and that is all averaging means.

## Now scale it up

| Replicates per group | σ̂² is this many times the perfect score | Gap |
|---:|---:|---|
| 2 | 2.000× | σ̂² / 2 |
| 3 | 1.500× | σ̂² / 3 |
| **10** | **1.111×** | **σ̂² / 10** |

The effect never disappears, it just shrinks. At ten replicates σ̂² is 11% larger than the perfect score. With a hundred replicates it would be 1% larger and nobody would mention it.

## The actual Cascade numbers

```
perfect model's test MSE  =  133.30
      × 10/9              =  148.11   ← this is exactly σ̂²
```

So σ̂² is not a different measurement from the perfect model's score. It is **that same score, multiplied by 10/9**. The brief tells you to subtract the inflated version from your model's score, so the result sits 14.81 too low. And 14.81 is both 133.30 / 9 and 148.11 / 10, the same number reached two ways.

**One sentence to hold on to:** σ̂² is deliberately inflated by 10/9 so that it estimates the *true* variance; a model's MSE is not inflated at all; subtracting one from the other leaves the inflation behind as a constant 14.81 offset.

Reporting it as asked is a complete answer and loses nothing.

Close, and the one place it slips is the important one.

**First half: correct.** σ̂² is the variance of `units` within a group of ten rows that are identical on every recorded field, pooled across the 45 groups.

**Second half: two fixes.**

**Fix 1, the big one. The perfect model does not predict the true y.** It cannot. The ten rows are identical on every field, so the model sees the same input ten times and must output the same number ten times. But the ten actual y values differ: 103, 118, 97, … There is no function of the features that can produce ten different outputs from ten identical inputs.

So the best any model can do is predict the group's **true mean demand**. And a model doing that still has error on every single row, because every row differs from the mean. If it could predict the true y, its MSE would be zero and there would be no noise floor at all. The floor exists precisely because it cannot.

**Fix 2, wording.** It is not "the variance of the perfect model." It is the **MSE of the perfect model**: average over the 450 rows of (actual y − predicted group mean)².

## And here is the thing that ties it together

Both numbers are built from **the same scatter**: how far the ten y values sit from their own mean. Same deviations, same squares, same sum. They differ only in what you divide by.

| | What it is | Divides the sum of squared deviations by |
|---|---|---|
| σ̂² = 148.11 | an *estimate of the true variance* | 9 per group (405 total) |
| perfect model's MSE = 133.30 | an *average over rows* | 10 per group (450 total) |

Not two different quantities. One quantity, counted two ways. 9 versus 10, hence 0.9, hence the 14.81 gap.

Why 9: to estimate a variance you had to work out where the mean was from those same ten numbers, which uses up one of them. Why 10: an average over rows divides by the number of rows, and there are ten.

**So the chain is:** the ten identical weeks differ → no model can predict which is which → the best possible model predicts the group mean and still has nonzero MSE → that MSE is 133.30 → σ̂² measures the same scatter but divides by 9 instead of 10, giving 148.11 → subtracting 148.11 instead of 133.30 takes out 14.81 too much.

Does the "same scatter, two divisors" framing do it? And either way, tell me: footnote in §2, or notes only?

Yes. That is exactly what it is.

σ̂² estimates Var(ε), the irreducible error: the variation in the outcome that remains when every recorded feature is held fixed. The replicate design is what makes it measurable. Ordinarily you cannot separate "the model is wrong" from "the outcome is noisy," because you only ever see each configuration once. Observing the same configuration ten times separates them, because anything that moves across those ten weeks cannot be explained by anything the model can see.

The one nuance, and it is the whole 10/9 business in a sentence:

- **σ̂² = 148.11** is the estimate of the irreducible error *as a property of the process*.
- **133.30** is the irreducible error *as it actually landed in these 450 rows*, averaged per row.

Same phenomenon. The first is the parameter estimate, corrected by the 9 so it is unbiased for the true variance. The second is a plain row average, dividing by 10. The brief has you subtract the first, so you over-subtract by the difference.

And that is why the brief insists a raw test MSE is uninterpretable. 246 units² means nothing on its own. 246 against a floor of 148 means about 60% of the remaining error is noise nobody can remove, and that is a sentence the VP can act on.

Footnote or notes only? Say the word and I draft B2 either way.

Yes, 154.15 is the reducible error: the part a better model could in principle remove.

**And it is exactly right, not conservative. I was wrong earlier.** Here is what I missed.

I said the brief's `test MSE − σ̂²` understates the removable error by σ̂²/10, because the "exact" gap against the replicate means is 168.36 rather than 154.15. But the replicate means are **themselves noisy**. Each one is an average of ten weeks, so it sits around the true mean with variance σ̂²/10 = 14.2. Measuring your model against a wobbly yardstick inflates the measured gap by exactly that wobble:

```
gap vs replicate means  =  true gap  +  σ̂²/10
       168.36                154.16      14.20
```

So the 168.36 I called exact was itself inflated by 14.2. Subtract the yardstick's own noise and you land on 154.16, which is the brief's quantity to rounding. Checked in all eight cells, matching to the cent.

**The two corrections cancel precisely.** The floor occupies only 405/450 of the test MSE, so subtracting a full σ̂² takes out σ̂²/10 too much. The replicate-mean gap is inflated by σ̂²/10 too much. Same quantity, opposite signs, exact cancellation. `test MSE − σ̂²` is the **unbiased estimate of the true average squared bias**, and the brief's instruction is not an approximation at all.

That settles the footnote question: there is nothing to footnote, because there is no shortfall to disclose. Report `test MSE − σ̂²` as asked, which `b2_table.csv` already does. If you want one optional sentence it is the pleasing version, that the quantity is exactly right because two offsetting biases cancel, but it costs words in a one-page section and the brief never claimed otherwise.

So for Apparel degree-2 ridge, the honest reading is: test MSE 296.19, of which about 142 is noise no model can touch and **154.15 is error a better model could in principle remove**. More than half the remaining error is still on the table even for the best of the four models, which is the headline for Apparel.

Drafting B2 now, with your three answers, the OLS-versus-ridge comparability clause kept short, and the CV-versus-test divergence.

The simulation settles it. 20,000 datasets where the true mean demand at each configuration is known by construction, so the true bias is known too:

| Quantity | Value | Verdict |
|---|---:|---|
| True average squared bias (the target) | 99.92 | what we want |
| Gap measured against replicate means | 114.19 | **overstates by 14.27** ≈ σ²/10 = 14.4 |
| The brief's `test MSE − σ̂²` | 99.79 | off by 0.13, Monte Carlo noise. **Unbiased** |

## Where I went wrong

I treated the replicate mean ȳ as if it were the truth. It is not. It is an average of ten noisy weeks, so it is an *estimate* of the true mean μ, and it misses by a random amount whose variance is σ²/10.

Three quantities, not two:

- **μ**, the true mean demand at a configuration. Unobservable.
- **ȳ**, the average of its ten observed weeks. Observable, but wobbly: Var(ȳ) = σ²/10.
- **ŷ**, the model's prediction.

What you want to know is how far the model is from **μ**. What you can compute is how far it is from **ȳ**. Those are different, and the difference is systematic.

## Why measuring against ȳ always overstates

Split the computable gap through the truth:

```
(ȳ − ŷ)  =  (ȳ − μ)  +  (μ − ŷ)
             wobble      true gap
```

Square it. The cross-term is 2 × wobble × true gap, and wobble averages to zero, so it drops out. What remains:

```
average of (ȳ − ŷ)²  =  σ²/10  +  average of (μ − ŷ)²
                        wobble²      what you want
```

The wobble contributes a **positive** amount no matter which direction it goes, because it is squared. So measuring against a noisy yardstick inflates the measured gap, always, by exactly the yardstick's variance. In the simulation: 114.19 against 99.92, inflated by 14.27, and σ²/10 = 14.4.

That was my error. I called 168.36 "the exact removable error" for Apparel degree-2 ridge. It is the removable error **plus 14.2 of yardstick noise**.

## Why the brief's quantity is exactly right

Two steps. From the decomposition:

```
test MSE  =  0.9·σ̂²  +  average of (ȳ − ŷ)²
```

so

```
test MSE − σ̂²  =  average of (ȳ − ŷ)²  −  σ̂²/10
```

And from the wobble argument, that is precisely how you convert the measured gap back into the true one:

```
average of (ȳ − ŷ)²  −  σ²/10  =  average of (μ − ŷ)²
```

So `test MSE − σ̂²` **is** the true average squared bias. The subtraction of a full σ̂² is not sloppy; it removes the 0.9σ̂² of real noise *and* the σ̂²/10 of yardstick noise in one stroke, because they add to exactly σ̂².

## The part that makes it feel inevitable

Both corrections are the same number, σ²/10, and both exist for the same reason: **you have ten replicates, not infinitely many.**

- With infinite replicates, the floor part would be a full σ̂² rather than 0.9σ̂², so there would be no over-subtraction.
- With infinite replicates, ȳ would equal μ exactly, so there would be no yardstick noise.

Both corrections vanish together as the group size grows. They are two faces of one finite-sample fact, which is why they cancel exactly rather than approximately. My mistake was keeping one of them and forgetting the other.

**The one-liner:** `test MSE − σ̂²` is unbiased for the reducible error, because the σ̂²/10 you over-subtract is exactly the σ̂²/10 of noise sitting in the replicate-mean yardstick.

"""
B3 -- the stability experiment.  The loop is written; the models are yours.

    python3 stability.py

What this measures.  A fitted model is a random object: it depends on which
training sample you happened to get (Handout 3 §2).  Refit the same procedure
on 20 bootstrap resamples of a division's extract, predict the same three
configurations each time, and you get 20 predictions per configuration.  Their
**spread** estimates variance.  The distance from their **average** to the
truth estimates bias.  The truth is available here because each query
configuration also appears in replicates.csv ten times, so the mean of those
ten estimates the true mean demand to within about sigma/sqrt(10) ~ 4 units.

What you have to do.  Fill in `build_models()` with the four B2 models, using
the penalties you tuned in B2 -- do NOT re-tune inside the resamples, or you
will be measuring something else.  Then read the output.

Everything else is fixed: 20 resamples from `bootstrap_indices()`, three query
points per division from `QUERY_CONFIGS`, the same seed for everyone.
"""
from pathlib import Path

import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from sklearn.linear_model import LinearRegression, Ridge  # noqa: F401

from load import (FEATURES, N_BOOTSTRAP, QUERY_CONFIGS, TARGET,
                  bootstrap_indices, dedup_h, load_a, load_h, load_replicates,
                  poly_pipeline, query_frame)

OUT = Path(__file__).resolve().parent / "b3_outputs"

# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# YOUR B2 PENALTIES.  Replace these with the values your own B2 tuning chose.
# They are per division and per degree, and they are not all the same number --
# at one of the two degrees the two divisions land in the same place, and at the
# other they do not.  Which is which is part of what B2 asked you to notice.
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
RIDGE_ALPHA_DEG1 = {"H": None, "A": None}
RIDGE_ALPHA_DEG2 = {"H": None, "A": None}


def build_models(division: str) -> dict:
    """The four B2 models, ready to fit.  Fill this in.

    Return a dict mapping a label to a fresh, unfitted estimator.  A fresh one
    every call -- if you build them once outside the loop, every resample will
    refit the same object and you will still get the right answer, but it is
    easy to get wrong in a way that silently reuses a fitted model.

    Use `poly_pipeline(estimator, degree=d)` so that the basis and the scaler
    are fitted on each resample rather than once on the whole extract.

    The four:
        degree-1 OLS     poly_pipeline(LinearRegression(), degree=1)
        degree-1 ridge   poly_pipeline(Ridge(alpha=...), degree=1)
        degree-2 OLS     poly_pipeline(LinearRegression(), degree=2)
        degree-2 ridge   poly_pipeline(Ridge(alpha=...), degree=2)
    """
    raise NotImplementedError("fill in build_models()")


# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Fixed below this line.
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

def truth_at_queries(division: str) -> np.ndarray:
    """Replicate-based estimate of true mean demand at the three query points."""
    r = load_replicates()
    r = r.loc[r["division"] == division]
    return (r.groupby("config_id")[TARGET].mean()
            .loc[QUERY_CONFIGS[division]].to_numpy())


def run(division: str, train: pd.DataFrame) -> pd.DataFrame:
    X = train[FEATURES].to_numpy()
    y = train[TARGET].to_numpy()
    Xq = query_frame(division).to_numpy()
    truth = truth_at_queries(division)

    draws = {label: [] for label in build_models(division)}
    for idx in bootstrap_indices(len(X), N_BOOTSTRAP):
        for label, model in build_models(division).items():
            model.fit(X[idx], y[idx])
            draws[label].append(model.predict(Xq))

    rows = []
    for label, preds in draws.items():
        P = np.asarray(preds)                       # (N_BOOTSTRAP, 3)
        sd = P.std(axis=0, ddof=1)                  # variance, per query point
        bias = P.mean(axis=0) - truth               # bias, per query point
        rows.append({
            "division": division,
            "model": label,
            **{f"sd_q{i + 1}": v for i, v in enumerate(sd)},
            **{f"bias_q{i + 1}": v for i, v in enumerate(bias)},
            "mean_sd": sd.mean(),
            "mean_abs_bias": np.abs(bias).mean(),
        })
    return pd.DataFrame(rows)


def figure(tables: pd.DataFrame) -> None:
    """One compact figure: mean spread against mean |bias|, by division."""
    import matplotlib
    matplotlib.use("Agg")
    import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

    fig, axes = plt.subplots(1, 2, figsize=(11, 4.2), sharey=False)
    for ax, d in zip(axes, ("H", "A")):
        t = tables.loc[tables.division == d]
        ax.scatter(t.mean_abs_bias, t.mean_sd)
        for _, r in t.iterrows():
            ax.annotate(r.model, (r.mean_abs_bias, r.mean_sd),
                        textcoords="offset points", xytext=(6, 4), fontsize=8)
        ax.set_xlabel("mean |bias| across the three query points")
        ax.set_ylabel("mean sd of the 20 predictions")
        ax.set_title({"H": "Hardgoods", "A": "Apparel"}[d])
    fig.suptitle("B3: what flexibility and what a penalty do to bias and variance")
    fig.tight_layout()
    OUT.mkdir(exist_ok=True)
    fig.savefig(OUT / "b3_stability.png", dpi=150)
    print(f"wrote {OUT / 'b3_stability.png'}")


def main() -> None:
    tables = pd.concat([run("H", dedup_h(load_h())), run("A", load_a())],
                       ignore_index=True)
    cols = ["division", "model", "mean_sd", "mean_abs_bias"]
    print(tables[cols].to_string(index=False, float_format=lambda v: f"{v:8.2f}"))
    OUT.mkdir(exist_ok=True)
    tables.to_csv(OUT / "b3_stability.csv", index=False)
    print(f"\nwrote {OUT / 'b3_stability.csv'}")
    figure(tables)
    print()
    print("Three questions, each answerable from a number in that table:")
    print("  1. What did raising the degree do to bias, and to variance,")
    print("     in each division?")
    print("  2. What did the penalty do to each, at each degree?")
    print("  3. The same four procedures, the same twelve fields, two")
    print("     divisions. Why is the answer not the same in both?")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Your reading is almost certainly right, but here is the full picture, because the sentence is doing more work than it looks.

## What B3 does mechanically

A fitted model is a **random object**. Not random in its predictions, but random in itself: fit the same procedure on a different sample of store-weeks and you get a different set of coefficients, and therefore a different prediction at the same query point.

B3 makes that randomness visible:

1. Take Hardgoods' 3,000 training rows. Draw a bootstrap resample, 3,000 rows sampled **with replacement**, so some rows appear twice and some not at all. That imitates "what if the planning system had handed us a different but equally valid sample."
2. Refit the procedure on that resample. Predict configuration H43.
3. Repeat 20 times. You now have 20 predictions for H43.

Those 20 numbers give you both quantities:

- **Variance:** their standard deviation. How much the prediction jumps around when the training data changes. Note this needs no outcome at all. It is a property of the procedure, not of any particular answer.
- **Bias:** their average, minus the truth. Where the procedure lands *on average*, versus where it should land.

And the three query points are low, middle and high demand, which the truths confirm: Hardgoods 88.98, 111.57, 132.87; Apparel 87.31, 107.03, 135.62.

## Why these two are normally inseparable

The decomposition is Err(x₀) = Bias² + Var + σ². In ordinary life you observe one week at x₀ and one fitted model, so you get a single squared error that mixes all three, and nothing tells you which part is which. If your prediction misses by 30 units you cannot say whether:

- the procedure is **systematically wrong** at that configuration (bias),
- this particular training sample gave you an **unlucky fit** (variance), or
- the model was fine and **that week was unusual** (noise).

The replicates break the tie twice over. Ten observations at an identical configuration give you the truth, which isolates bias. Twenty refits give you the spread, which isolates variance. Neither is available without the controlled design.

## The sentence itself

> the mean of its ten replicates estimates the true mean demand there to within about σ/√10 ≈ 4 units

The truth μ is unknowable; you substitute ȳ, the average of ten observed weeks. Averaging ten draws shrinks their scatter by √10, so the standard error of that yardstick is σ/√10: **3.85 units in Hardgoods, 3.77 in Apparel**.

That is a **precision statement about the ruler**, and it has a direct practical consequence for reading your own B3 table: a measured bias of 2 or 3 units is indistinguishable from zero, because the ruler itself wobbles by about 4. A measured bias of 25 units is real. The brief's "if one of these effects is nearly zero, say so and give the number" is pointing at exactly this, and ~4 units is the yardstick for "nearly zero."

Note that this is the same fact we just worked through, written in standard deviations instead of variances. There σ²/10 inflated the measured squared gap; here σ/√10 is its square root, the typical size of the wobble. One phenomenon, two notations.

> That is what lets you see bias and variance separately rather than only their sum

"Their sum" is the reducible error, exactly what B2 reports as `test MSE − σ̂²`. B2 tells you **how much** error a better model could remove. B3 tells you **which kind** it is, and therefore what to do about it:

| What B3 finds | What it means | The fix |
|---|---|---|
| Large bias, small variance | the model class is too rigid to represent the demand surface | more flexibility: raise the degree |
| Small bias, large variance | the model is capable but the sample cannot pin it down | more data, or a penalty |

That is the whole argument of §2. B2 showed Apparel leaves 154 units² recoverable while Hardgoods leaves 99. B3 says whether Apparel's 154 is a rigidity problem or a sample-size problem, and the answer is what the company-wide standard should be written to address. My expectation before running, so it is on record: Apparel's problem is variance, and that is why the penalty bought 288 there and nothing anywhere else.

Building it now.

A **query point** is just one specific input where you ask the model for a prediction. "Query" as in querying the model: here is a row of twelve field values, what do you predict?

In this data a query point is one of the 45 configurations: a particular combination of promo depth, own price index, competitor price, marketing spend, temperature, loyalty share, store size, inventory cover, assortment breadth, catalog pages, returns rate and staffing hours. `query_frame("H")` hands back three such rows, twelve columns each. `QUERY_CONFIGS` names them: H43, H16, H01 for Hardgoods, A10, A08, A07 for Apparel.

Why they matter here: **bias and variance are defined at a point**, not globally. Handout 3 writes Err(x₀), with the x₀ there for a reason. The same model can be rigid in one corner of feature space and jumpy in another, so to measure variance you must hold the input fixed and vary only the training data. The query point is what you hold fixed.

The procedure is then: pick a configuration, say H43. Fit the model on resample 1, ask it about H43, write down the answer. Fit on resample 2, ask about H43 again, write down the answer. Twenty times. Those twenty answers to the *same question* are what have a spread and a mean.

An analogy. You want to know how reliable a weather model is. You pick a specific question, "high temperature in Chicago next Tuesday." That question is the query point. Then you train twenty versions of the model on twenty slightly different historical datasets and ask all twenty the same question. If they answer 71, 73, 70, 72, … they agree and the procedure has low variance. If they answer 58, 84, 65, 79, … the procedure is unstable. And if all twenty cluster tightly around 72 while the real answer turns out to be 85, they agree with each other and are collectively wrong, which is bias.

Three points rather than all 45 because each one needs a known truth and twenty refits, and three spanning low, middle and high demand is enough to see whether the story changes with demand level. The brief fixes which three so everyone's numbers compare.

Building B3 now.

B3 runs in 2 seconds and the prediction held. Results, then one finding that changes the §2 story.

## Exhibit B3

| Division | Model | Mean sd | Mean \|bias\| | Signed bias |
|---|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Hardgoods | degree-1 OLS | 1.14 | 12.65 | +1.35 |
| Hardgoods | degree-1 ridge | 1.14 | 12.66 | +1.34 |
| Hardgoods | degree-2 OLS | 2.83 | 3.84 | +0.86 |
| Hardgoods | degree-2 ridge | 2.80 | 3.64 | +1.05 |
| Apparel | degree-1 OLS | 4.33 | 16.66 | +9.35 |
| Apparel | degree-1 ridge | 4.13 | 16.46 | +8.70 |
| Apparel | degree-2 OLS | **25.24** | 18.78 | −6.16 |
| Apparel | degree-2 ridge | **7.22** | 15.05 | +4.15 |

**The degree-1 prediction confirmed.** Hardgoods 1.14 → 1.14 and 12.65 → 12.66; Apparel 4.33 → 4.13 and 16.66 → 16.46. The penalty does nothing at degree 1 in either division, exactly as the flat α curves implied. That is the brief's "if one of these effects is nearly zero, say so and give the number," and the number is 0.01 units of standard deviation.

**Raising the degree does opposite things in the two divisions.** In Hardgoods it is the textbook trade: variance up from 1.14 to 2.83, bias down from 12.65 to 3.84, and the bias reduction wins. In Apparel it is not a trade at all: variance explodes from 4.33 to 25.24, nearly six-fold, and bias does not improve, going 16.66 to 18.78. You pay and get nothing. That is why degree-2 OLS scored 618 in B2.

**The penalty rescues precisely that cell.** Apparel degree 2: variance 25.24 → 7.22, a 71% cut, and bias also improves slightly, 18.78 → 15.05. Same 90 terms, same procedure, and the only difference from Hardgoods is 197 rows against 3,000, which is question 3's answer: terms per row, 0.46 against 0.03, the same quantity that explained B2.

**Signed versus absolute matters, and you were right to want both.** Hardgoods degree-1 OLS biases are +21.01, −15.06, −1.89. The signed mean is +1.35, which would look like an unbiased model. It is not; it is a rigid surface that over-predicts low demand by 21 and under-predicts middle demand by 15, and the errors merely cancel. Degree 2 flattens that to −4.46, +5.01, +2.05.

## The finding that changes the story

I predicted Apparel's problem would be variance. Converting to comparable units²:

| Division | Model | Bias² | Variance | Total | % bias |
|---|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| Hardgoods | degree-2 ridge | 15.1 | 8.9 | 24.0 | 63% |
| Apparel | degree-2 OLS | 474.9 | 664.2 | 1139.0 | 42% |
| Apparel | degree-2 ridge | 292.8 | 52.9 | 345.7 | **85%** |

For the unpenalised degree-2 Apparel fit I was right: variance is the majority. But **the penalty converts it into a bias problem.** Apparel's best model carries 293 units² of bias² against 53 of variance. Its remaining recoverable error is overwhelmingly systematic, not jumpiness, and a mean absolute bias of 15.05 against a 3.77-unit yardstick is unambiguously real.

That matters for what you tell the VP. "Apparel is unstable, penalise it" is only half the story. The penalty has already done its job; what is left is a model too rigid to follow the Apparel demand surface, and the thing that would fix it is more Apparel history, not a different estimator. The standard should say so.

**One caveat to carry into the draft.** These are three query points chosen to span low, middle and high demand, so they are harder than average, and bootstrap resamples contain about 63% unique rows so they fit slightly worse than the full-data refit. Both inflate B3's totals relative to B2's 45-configuration average: 345.7 here against 154.15 there for the same cell. The directions and the comparisons are sound; the levels are not interchangeable with B2's.

Want me to draft B3 now with that reading, or do you want to push back on the bias conclusion first?

ASSISTANT  [2026-10-01T07:29:12.006Z]
==============================================================================

They are the same quantities. The second table is just **squared**, and that is most of what you are seeing.

## The big factor: units

Table 1 is in **units**. Table 2 is in **units²**.

Apparel degree-2 OLS, the three query points have sd 18.08, 30.25, 27.40. Average: 25.24, which is table 1. Square each one and you get 327.0, 914.8, 750.7, averaging 664.2, which is table 2. A number near 25 becomes a number near 650 when squared. That is the whole jump.

## Why squared at all

Because the bias-variance decomposition only adds up in squared units:

```
Err  =  Bias²  +  Variance  +  σ²
```

You cannot add "18.78 units of bias" to "25.24 units of spread." They are not on the same scale, any more than you can add a length to an area. To say "variance is 58% of the total" you have to put both in units², which means squaring the bias and squaring the standard deviation. Variance *is* the squared standard deviation; that is its definition.

It also puts B3 on the same scale as everything else in Part B: σ̂² = 142.04 units², and B2's `test MSE − σ̂²` = 154.15 units². Table 1's 25.24 cannot be compared to those. Table 2's 664.2 can.

## The small factor: averaging before or after squaring

A secondary wrinkle, worth knowing because it is the same Jensen effect that appears all over this course:

| | Apparel degree-2 OLS |
|---|---:|
| Square of the mean sd, 25.24² | 637.2 |
| Mean of the squared sds | **664.2** |
| Square of the mean \|bias\|, 18.78² | 352.7 |
| Mean of the squared biases | **474.9** |

Squaring first and then averaging gives a larger number, because squaring penalises the spread between the three points. The bias case shows it strongly: the three biases are +18.94, −5.17, −32.24, so one point is far worse than the others and squaring magnifies it.

Table 2 uses mean-of-squares, and that is the correct order. The decomposition holds **at each query point separately** (Handout 3 writes Err(x₀)), so you decompose at each point and then average the decomposition. Averaging the biases first and squaring afterwards would be decomposing something that is not the error at any point.

## So, the three ways to read the same row

| Apparel degree-2 OLS | Value | Use |
|---|---:|---|
| Mean sd | 25.24 units | "typical prediction moves by 25 units" — what the brief asks for |
| Mean \|bias\| | 18.78 units | "typically off by 19 units" — comparable to the 3.77-unit yardstick |
| Variance / Bias² | 664.2 / 474.9 units² | the only form in which the two can be compared or added |

Table 1 is what the brief asks for and what goes in the exhibit. Table 2 exists solely so I could say "85% of Apparel's best model's recoverable error is bias," which is a statement about proportions and therefore needs squared units.

Does that clear it? If so, I will draft B3 with the exhibit in the brief's units and the bias-share point carried in one sentence with its units² figures.

Each model refit on every resample and asked for the same three\nquery configurations (low, middle and high demand). \"sd\" is the spread of the\ntwenty predictions; \"bias\" is their mean minus the replicate-based truth.\nAveraged over the three points.\n\n| Division | Model | Mean sd | Mean \\|bias\\| | Signed mean bias |\n|---|---|---:|---:|---:|\n| Hardgoods | degree-1 OLS | 1.14 | 12.65 | +1.35 |\n| Hardgoods | degree-1 ridge | 1.14 | 12.66 | +1.34 |\n| Hardgoods | degree-2 OLS | 2.83 | 3.84 | +0.86 |\n| Hardgoods | degree-2 ridge | **2.80** | **3.64** | +1.05 |\n| Apparel | degree-1 OLS | 4.33 | 16.66 | +9.35 |\n| Apparel | degree-1 ridge | 4.13 | 16.46 | +8.70 |\n| Apparel | degree-2 OLS | **25.24** | 18.78 | −6.16 |\n| Apparel | degree-2 ridge | **7.22** | **15.05** | +4.15 |\n\nAll figures in units. The truth at each query point is itself an average of\nten weeks, so it carries a standard error of σ̂/√10 — 3.85 units in\nHardgoods, 3.77 in Apparel. A bias smaller than that is indistinguishable\nfrom zero.\n\n### Per query point (q1 low, q2 middle, q3 high demand)\n\n| Division | Model | sd q1 | sd q2 | sd q3 | bias q1 | bias q2 | bias q3 |\n|---|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|\n| Hardgoods | degree-1 OLS | 1.02 | 1.15 | 1.25 | **+21.01** | **−15.06** | −1.89 |\n| Hardgoods | degree-2 OLS | 1.97 | 4.33 | 2.18 | −4.46 | +5.01 | +2.05 |\n| Hardgoods | degree-2 ridge | 1.96 | 4.28 | 2.16 | −3.89 | +5.15 | +1.88 |\n| Apparel | degree-1 OLS | 3.39 | 5.07 | 4.52 | +19.60 | +19.40 | −10.97 |\n| Apparel | degree-2 OLS | 18.08 | **30.25** | 27.40 | +18.94 | −5.17 | **−32.24** |\n| Apparel | degree-2 ridge | 5.96 | 7.62 | 8.07 | +24.31 | +4.48 | −16.35 |\n\n(Degree-1 ridge omitted here: identical to degree-1 OLS to two decimals in\nboth divisions. Full table in the CSV.)\n\n---\n\n## Prose (draft, ~170 words)\n\n**What raising the degree did.** In Hardgoods it bought the trade it is\nsupposed to: the spread of the predictions rose from 1.14 units to 2.83 while\nthe typical distance from the truth fell from 12.65 to 3.84, so a small\namount of instability purchased a large correction. In Apparel it bought\nnothing: the spread rose from 4.33 to 25.24, nearly six-fold, and the\ndistance from the truth did not improve at all, moving from 16.66 to 18.78.\n\n**What the penalty did.** Nothing at degree 1, in either division — 1.14 to\n1.14 in Hardgoods and 4.33 to 4.13 in Apparel, with bias unchanged to within\n0.2 units. At degree 2 it is nothing in Hardgoods (2.83 to 2.80) and\ndecisive in Apparel, cutting the spread from 25.24 to 7.22, a 71% reduction,\nwhile also improving bias slightly.\n\n**Why the same four procedures answer differently.** Ninety terms estimated\nfrom 3,000 rows is 0.03 terms per row; from 197 rows it is 0.46. That ratio,\nnot the division's size, is what B2 found separates the cell where the\npenalty is worth 288 units² of cross-validated error from the three where it\nis worth nothing, and it is the same ratio that makes Apparel's degree-2\npredictions move nine times as much as Hardgoods'.\n\n---\n\n## Notes to self (not memo text)\n\n### Design decisions (mine, session 6)\n\n- **The supplied loop runs as issued.** `analysis/part2/b3_stability.py`\n  imports `part2_cascade/starter/stability.py` rather than copying or editing\n  it, and supplies the three things it leaves blank: the two penalty dicts,\n  `build_models()`, and the output path. `git diff` against the supplied\n  folder stays empty. Defensible in one sentence: the measurement is the\n  instructor's, only the four models are mine.\n- **Penalties read from `b2_penalties.csv`,** not retyped, so they cannot\n  drift from B2 and cannot be re-tuned inside the resamples.\n- **Output to `analysis/outputs/`** as `b3_stability.csv` and `.png`, dropping\n  the starter's `b3_outputs/` folder. This **overrides the session-1 decision**\n  to keep the starter's folder names; one consistent output location won, and\n  B1 and B2 already write there.\n- **Signed mean bias added** as a column the starter does not compute. It\n  earns its place: see the cancellation point below.\n- **sd reported, not sd².** The brief asks for the standard deviation. Squared\n  units appear only where proportions are being compared, and then they are\n  computed as mean-of-squares, not square-of-mean.\n\n### An independent check the starter embeds\n\n`stability.py`'s comment on the penalties says \"at one of the two degrees the\ntwo divisions land in the same place, and at the other they do not.\" Ours:\ndegree 1 is α = 10.0 in **both** divisions, degree 2 is 0.316 (H) against\n3.162 (A). Hint satisfied. Second embedded check Part B has passed, after\nσ̂/√10 ≈ 4 in B1. Both are worth one line in the process note.\n\n(Consequence: my B2 note calling the degree-1 α \"close to arbitrary\" was too\nstrong and has been softened. The curve is flat — 19 of 25 α values within\none SE of the minimum in Hardgoods, 16 of 25 in Apparel — but both divisions\nfinding the same minimum is evidently expected, not luck.)\n\n### Why signed bias was worth adding\n\nHardgoods degree-1 OLS: biases of **+21.01, −15.06, −1.89**. Mean absolute\n12.65, signed mean **+1.35**. Reported signed alone, that model looks nearly\nunbiased. It is not: it is a surface too rigid to bend, over-predicting low\ndemand by 21 units and under-predicting middle demand by 15, and the two\nmerely cancel in the average. Degree 2 flattens it to −4.46, +5.01, +2.05.\nMean absolute is the right headline; signed mean is the diagnostic that\nreveals the shape. One clause in §2 if the budget allows.\n\n### The finding that changes §2's conclusion\n\nConverting to units² so bias and variance can be compared (mean of squares at\neach query point, because the decomposition holds at each point):\n\n| Division | Model | Bias² | Variance | Total | Bias share |\n|---|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|\n| Hardgoods | degree-1 OLS | 224.0 | 1.3 | 225.3 | 99% |\n| Hardgoods | degree-2 ridge | 15.1 | 8.9 | 24.0 | 63% |\n| Apparel | degree-1 OLS | 293.6 | 19.2 | 312.9 | 94% |\n| Apparel | degree-2 OLS | 474.9 | 664.2 | 1139.0 | 42% |\n| Apparel | degree-2 ridge | **292.8** | **52.9** | 345.7 | **85%** |\n\nI predicted before running that Apparel's problem would be variance. For the\nunpenalised degree-2 fit that is right — variance is the majority, 664 of\n1,139. But **the penalty converts it into a bias problem**: Apparel's best\nmodel carries 293 units² of bias² against 53 of variance. A mean absolute\nbias of 15.05 units against a 3.77-unit yardstick is unambiguously real.\n\nSo \"Apparel is unstable, penalise it\" is only half the story. The penalty has\nalready done its work; what remains is a model too rigid to follow the\nApparel demand surface, and the fix is more Apparel history, not a different\nestimator. That is what the company-wide standard should say, and it is the\nbridge from B3 into the standard-a-process answer.\n\n### Arguing against the draft\n\n- **Twenty resamples is few.** A standard deviation from 20 draws has roughly\n  16% relative uncertainty of its own. So 2.83 vs 2.80 in Hardgoods degree 2\n  is not a finding, and neither is 4.33 vs 4.13 in Apparel degree 1. Both are\n  reported as \"nothing\", which is the honest reading, and that is also what\n  B2's paired CV differences said (0.15 ± 0.22 and 1.47 ± 7.26).\n- **B3's levels are not comparable with B2's.** Apparel degree-2 ridge is\n  345.7 units² here against B2's 154.15. Two reasons, both real: these three\n  query points are deliberately spread to low/middle/high demand and are\n  harder than the 45-configuration average, and a bootstrap resample contains\n  only about 63% unique rows so every fit is slightly worse than the\n  full-data refit. Directions and comparisons hold; levels do not transfer.\n  **Do not put 345.7 and 154.15 in the same sentence of the memo.**\n- **The bootstrap measures variance over resamples, not over genuinely new\n  samples.** It is the standard proxy and the brief prescribes it, but a\n  resample is drawn from the one extract we have, so it cannot see variation\n  the extract itself does not contain.\n- **Bias here is measured against a replicate mean, not the truth.** Same\n  σ̂/√10 wobble as everywhere else in Part B; ~3.8 units. It is why \"nearly\n  zero\" has to mean \"under about 4\", not \"under 1\".\n- **Three query points is three.** They were chosen by the instructor to span\n  the demand range, which is the right design, but no claim about how bias\n  behaves *across* feature space can rest on three points. The per-point\n  signs are suggestive (both divisions over-predict low demand and\n  under-predict high), not established.\n- **Apparel degree-2 ridge still has the largest bias of any Apparel model\n  bar degree-2 OLS**, and its signed mean is +4.15 while its per-point\n  biases run +24.31, +4.48, −16.35. It is the best of the four and still not\n  a good model. Say that plainly rather than letting \"the penalty fixed it\"\n  stand unqualified.\n\n### Numbers used and where they live\n\n| Number | File |\n|---|---|\n| All eight rows: sd and bias at each query point, mean sd, mean \\|bias\\|, signed mean | `b3_stability.csv` |\n| The figure | `b3_stability.png` |\n| Penalties 10.0 / 10.0 / 0.316 / 3.162 | `b2_penalties.csv` |\n| σ̂/√10 = 3.85 / 3.77 | `b1_noise_floor.csv`, printed in `run.log` |\n| Terms per row 0.03 / 0.46; penalty worth 288 ± 55 | `b2_table.csv`, `b2_differences.csv` |\n| Bias² / variance split (units²) | computed in session from `b3_stability.csv`; **needs adding to the script before any of it goes in the memo** |\n"

## B3 is done

[drafts/b3_draft.md](drafts/b3_draft.md), with Exhibit B3 in the brief's units, a per-query-point table, 170 words of prose answering the three questions, and notes.

**`run.sh` now runs the whole submission in 18 seconds**, A1 through B3. The supplied folders are still byte-identical to what was issued, which B3 was the real test of: [analysis/part2/b3_stability.py](analysis/part2/b3_stability.py) imports `stability.py` and supplies only the two penalty dicts, `build_models()` and the output path, so the instructor's loop runs as written.

**The bias²/variance split is now computed by the script** rather than in conversation, so it is quotable. `b3_stability.csv` carries `bias2_mean`, `variance_mean` and `bias_share_pct`.

## The three answers

| | Hardgoods | Apparel |
|---|---|---|
| Degree 1 → 2 | the textbook trade: sd 1.14 → 2.83, bias 12.65 → 3.84 | no trade: sd 4.33 → **25.24**, bias 16.66 → 18.78, worse |
| Penalty at degree 1 | nothing: 1.14 → 1.14 | nothing: 4.33 → 4.13 |
| Penalty at degree 2 | nothing: 2.83 → 2.80 | decisive: 25.24 → **7.22**, a 71% cut |
| Why different | 0.03 terms per row | 0.46 terms per row |

The degree-1 prediction I put on record before running held to 0.01 units, and the starter's own hint about the penalties landing in the same place at one degree and not the other is satisfied, which is the second embedded check Part B has passed.

## Two things in the notes you should read before the memo

**The conclusion moved.** I predicted Apparel's problem was variance. For degree-2 OLS that is right, 664 of 1,139 units². But the penalty converts it: Apparel's best model is **85% bias**, 293 units² against 53. So "Apparel is unstable, penalise it" is half the story. The penalty has done its job, and what remains is a surface too rigid for the Apparel demand curve. The fix is more Apparel history, not a different estimator, and that is the bridge into "standardise a process, not a model."

**One hard rule for the memo:** do not put B3's 345.7 and B2's 154.15 in the same sentence. Same cell, different scales, because the three query points are deliberately harder than average and bootstrap resamples hold only about 63% unique rows. Directions compare, levels do not.

## Where §2 stands on length

B1 62 + B2 392 + B3 170 = **624 words against a one-page budget of roughly 450 to 500.** About 150 words need to come out, and the candidates are already marked: B2's fourth paragraph, the signed-bias clause, and the CV-versus-test divergence. That trim is best done when you write the memo and can see §1 and §2 together.

ASSISTANT  [2026-10-01T07:38:04.939Z]
==============================================================================

The two numbers measure the same *concept* for the same *cell*, but on two different populations of points and from two different training-set sizes. The gap decomposes cleanly:

| Quantity | Value | What changed |
|---|---:|---|
| B2: one fit on all 197 rows, averaged over **all 45** configurations | **154.2** | the reported number |
| Same fit, averaged over the **3 query points only** | **257.3** | which points |
| B3: **20 bootstrap fits**, same 3 points | **345.7** | which fits |

## Step one: which points, 154 → 257

The three query points are not a sample of the 45. The brief chose them to span low, middle and high demand, which is the right design for seeing whether behaviour changes with demand level, but it means two of the three sit at the extremes of the range, where a rigid surface fits worst. Per point, the reducible error of the single full-data fit is A10 = 624, A08 = −12, A07 = 159. The middle point is essentially perfect; the low-demand point is catastrophic.

So averaging over three points that include the worst corner gives 257, while averaging over all 45, most of which are mid-range, gives 154. Nothing is wrong with either; they answer "how bad is this model at these three places" and "how bad is it on average."

The negative value at A08 is worth noticing: −12 is below the floor, which cannot be true of a real quantity. It is the σ̂²/10 correction overshooting at a single point where the model happens to be very accurate. The subtraction is unbiased *on average over configurations*, not guaranteed non-negative at any one of them.

## Step two: which fits, 257 → 346

B2 fits once on all 197 rows. B3 refits on bootstrap resamples, and a resample of size n drawn with replacement contains only about 1 − 1/e ≈ 63% distinct rows. Measured here: **0.625, about 123 of the 197 rows are distinct** in a typical resample.

So every B3 fit is trained on roughly 123 effective rows rather than 197, estimating 90 terms. That makes each fit worse, and it inflates both components. The extra 88 units² between 257 and 346 is the price of that, and it is exactly the Apparel-size effect the whole section is about, which is why it shows up here and would barely register in Hardgoods.

## Why the rule follows

Putting 345.7 and 154.15 in one sentence invites the reader to subtract them, and that difference means nothing: it is two unrelated changes stacked on each other. Worse, it would imply B3 contradicts B2, when they agree.

What *is* safe is every comparison **within** a table, because those hold the points and the fits fixed and change only the model:

- Within B3: Apparel degree 2, variance 664.2 → 52.9 when the penalty is applied. Both over the same three points and the same resampling scheme.
- Within B2: Apparel degree 2, CV 617.97 → 329.80, and test − σ̂² 224.82 → 154.15. All over the same 45 configurations and the same single-fit protocol.

So: B2 for levels, B3 for mechanism. Never a level from one against a level from the other.

Now closing out the session.
